# Fibres of Fine-Tuning

## A relative theory of parameter-efficient fine-tuning

*The final framework, synthesised from four proposals (Para and lenses; the relative point of view; Erlangen and gauge; string diagrams) and three referee reports. Every disputed claim was re-derived. A further round of skeptical referees then checked every numbered result against its proof, the literature and the Hugging Face PEFT source; their verdicts are applied here, and the per-result record (status and referee notes) is in `theory/propositions.json`. A second adversarial round, with a mathematical and a faithfulness referee for each repaired result (`theory/round2_verdicts.json`), has also been applied. Each result in `propositions.json` now carries a `round2` field (`verified`: no major problem, the statement stood and only wording, scope or commentary changed; `repaired`: a major problem or a refuted clause was fixed; `not-reviewed`: outside round 2), a `round2_notes` summary and a `public_statement`. The numerical claims were re-checked by `theory/framework_checks.py` (sections 1–11; section 12 re-checks the main numbers introduced in the first referee round and section 13 those of the second; figures marked "referee replication", and the remaining ones, come from the referees' own scripts) and by the proposals' own check scripts. On 3 October 2026 the statements and proofs were synchronised with the refereed paper; each result that changed records what changed in a `paper_sync` field of `propositions.json`.*

### How to read this document

**Status labels.**

| Label | Meaning |
|---|---|
| [Def] | definition |
| [Thm], [Prop], [Lemma], [Cor] | proved here |
| [Obs] | true and formally easy; kept because it organises |
| [Known: …] | due to the cited authors; we re-derive it in our language and add only the PEFT reading |
| [Num] | checked numerically (float64 autograd Jacobian ranks, exact optimizer runs) |
| [Sketch] | proof sketch only |
| [Pred] | falsifiable prediction |
| [Conj] | conjecture |
| [Analogy] | organising language with no theorem behind it |

**Conventions.** These carry real weight in the arguments below.

- *Linear box.* A linear box computes $y=Wx$ with $W\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}$, $m=d_{\rm out}$, $n=d_{\rm in}$. Row $i$ of $W$ is output neuron $i$.
- *Two Gram matrices.* $K_{\rm out}(W)=WW^\top$ is the neuron Gram matrix and $K_{\rm in}(W)=W^\top W$ the input Gram matrix. A right multiplication $W\mapsto WR$ with $R\in O(n)$ (the *input side*) fixes $K_{\rm out}$. A left multiplication $W\mapsto PW$ fixes $K_{\rm in}$. Papers that store $W^\top$ swap these words; we translate every method into the convention above.
- *Rank varieties.* $\mathcal M_{\le r}$ is the variety of matrices of rank at most $r$ and $\mathcal M_r$ its smooth stratum of rank exactly $r$. Then $\dim\mathcal M_r=r(m+n-r)$ and $\mathrm{Sing}\,\mathcal M_{\le r}=\mathcal M_{\le r-1}$.
- *LoRA.* $\rho(B,A)=W_0+sBA$ with $B\in\mathbb R^{m\times r}$ and $A\in\mathbb R^{r\times n}$, and $G=\nabla_WL$. The group $GL_r$ acts by $g\cdot(B,A)=(Bg^{-1},gA)$.
- *Groups.* $T_m$ is the diagonal torus (invertible diagonal matrices), $T_m^+=\{\mathrm{diag}(d):d_i>0\}$ its identity component, $\mathrm{Diag}_m$ the monoid of all diagonal matrices, $\mathrm{Mon}_m=T_m\rtimes S_m$ the monomial matrices, $B_r=\{\pm1\}^r\rtimes S_r$ the signed permutations, and $CO(n)=\mathbb R_{>0}\cdot O(n)$ the conformal orthogonal group ($CO^+(2)\cong\mathbb C^\times$).
- *Ambient category.* Maps and dynamics live in $\mathbf{Diff}$. Images are taken in $\mathbf{Set}$. Every image we meet is semialgebraic, or definable in $\mathbb R_{\exp}$ when softmax is involved, so dimensions, smooth points and tangent cones are well defined.

---

## Exposé 0. Thesis

Fine-tuning moves a point. A pretrained checkpoint $\theta_0$ is a **base point**, and a PEFT method is a **relative object over it**: a pointed map
$$\rho:(Q,q_0)\longrightarrow(\Theta,\theta_0)$$
from a small space of trainable parameters into weight space. Following Grothendieck, we study the morphism and not the object. Almost all the work is done by three invariants of the morphism, plus one further layer for architectures.

1. **The image germ at $\theta_0$ governs expressivity.** The germ is a cone, a linear space, a group orbit or a torus saturation. The relevant data are its first-order image $S_1$, whether $\theta_0$ is a smooth point or an apex, its cut-rank and its dimension (Exposé II).
2. **The fibres, together with a metric, govern dynamics.** Fibres are gauge orbits. Combined with the optimizer's metric they induce a cometric $K=D\rho\,\Lambda\,D\rho^\top$ on weight space. For LoRA with per-block rates, the compact half of the gauge gives isometries and the non-compact half gives conserved charges under gradient flow. The optimizer's equivariance group decides what descends to the model (Exposé III). The lens structure of backpropagation sorts tangent-side from cotangent-side methods (Exposé IV).
3. **Base change moves $\theta_0$.** This covers initialisation by splitting, quantisation, restarts and merging (Exposé V).
4. **Extensions and mergeability** are the only place where the network $f$ itself enters. Adapters and prompts first *extend* $f$. Merging is then a lifting problem, certified by a local rewriting calculus that gives sufficient conditions and box-local obstructions (Exposé VI).

**Headline results.** Each is proved below; the scope stated here is the scope of the proof.

- *LoRA+ is $\alpha$ in disguise.* For zero-initialised LoRA under Adam with $\varepsilon=0$, no weight decay, no gradient clipping, a learning-rate schedule shared by all parameter groups and the same data order and dropout masks, LoRA+ with ratio $\lambda$ follows *exactly* (in exact arithmetic) the trajectory of plain LoRA with $\alpha$ multiplied by $\lambda$. Per-block $\varepsilon$ and decoupled weight decay keep the statement once they are rescaled too. The LoRA/Adam case is due to Schulman et al. (2025); we add the general multihomogeneous and SGD forms (Thm III.10, Cor III.11).
- *No exact pointing buys rank above $2r$* (Thm II.1). The three full-rank strata of LoRA initialisations are classified, as a set, by $\mathrm{Gr}(r,n)\sqcup\mathrm{Gr}(r,m)\sqcup\mathcal M_r$; degenerate pointings, including HF's `orthogonal` init, lie outside them (Thm II.2).
- *Zero-init LoRA starts at the apex of a cone, and so does HRA's paired initialisation* for injective $W_0$ and even $r\le n-2$ (Thm II.2, Thm II.7).
- *$\text{LoRA-XS}=\mathrm{FA}\times\mathrm{FB}$ is a categorical product* in the SVD frames of $W_0$, when $\operatorname{rank}W_0\ge r$ (Prop I.8). *For injective $W_0$ and even $r\le n-2$, the image of HRA without Gram–Schmidt (HF's default) is the meet of LoRA's image with the rotation orbit $W_0\cdot SO(n)$*: an image-level meet, not a product of methods (Thm II.7).
- *Exactly orthogonal methods can never change the spectrum, however often they are merged* (Cor II.6, Thm V.3), in exact arithmetic. This covers exact-Cayley OFT, strict GOFT without its default scaler, and HRA. HF PEFT's default Cayley–Neumann OFT is only approximately orthogonal, and with small parameters each merge shrinks neuron norms and singular values; low-precision merges add rounding drift.
- *Rank is a cut, dimension is a volume.* At equal budget and when $2r^2<m+n-1$, LoHa$_{r,r}$ gives up $(m+n-1)-2r^2$ dimensions relative to LoRA$_{2r}$ (about $m+n$ when $r^2\ll(m+n)/2$); for $r\ge3$ it gains rank up to $r^2$ in exchange, and for $r\le2$ it is strictly dominated. When $2r^2\ge m+n-1$ the dimension bound no longer separates the two, and wherever it is attained, as the numerical checks find, LoHa matches or beats LoRA$_{2r}$ on both counts. GraLoRA keeps LoRA's dimension and raises the maximum rank to $\min(kr,m,n)$, but caps every block at rank $r/k$, so neither image contains the other (Prop II.12).
- *On LoRA's full-rank locus, Adam sees only a finite shadow, $B_r$, of the $GL_r$ gauge* (Thm III.12).
- *GaLore is one-sided LoRA with a moving frame*, with optimizer state carried across re-basings, exactly when weight decay is $0$ and the optimizer reads only its gradients (Prop IV.2; Torroba-Hennigen et al. 2025).
- *Merging is a lifting problem with explicit side conditions.* (IA)$^3$ always fuses into $W_{\rm down}$; whether it can fuse into $W_{\rm up}$ instead depends on the activation: yes under SwiGLU for every scale, no under GELU (Thm VI.3; the same scale migration appears in AWQ's code). The rewrite rules give sufficient conditions, not a decision procedure.

**Where the category theory earns its keep.** Seven places:
1. the pointed slice gives the interval [frozen, full FT] for weight-space methods and makes "simulation" precise;
2. isomorphism invariants finer than the image ($S_1$, the singular locus) classify initialisations;
3. a categorical product produces a named method (LoRA-XS), and image-level meets organise others (HRA);
4. base change separates function-preserving reparametrisations, which act on the slice, from maps that move the base point (quantisers); the resulting pointing defects are elementary, but the slice makes their order dependence explicit;
5. the lens 2-functor $\mathbf{Para}(\mathsf R)$ separates tangent-side from cotangent-side methods, and the degree-one collapse shows that every cotangent method whose optimizer reads only its gradients (no weight decay, no parameter scaling, no full-gradient clipping) is, period by period, a forward linear method on a moving base;
6. naturality: gradient descent is natural exactly where cometrics agree (for LoRA's linear gauge with block-scalar rates, along $O(r)$ where $A$ has full row rank and $r<n$), and combination rules must be gauge-natural;
7. symmetric monoidal structure turns rank into a min-cut.

The rest is real algebraic geometry, invariant theory, Lie generation and gradient-flow ODEs, and we say so where it happens. True but idle constructions (coends for the rank filtration, a saturation monad, sheaf language for locality, the Grothendieck construction $\int\mathsf{Meth}\to\mathbf{PNet}$, coKleisli 2-cells for mixtures) are omitted.

---

## Exposé I. The pointed slice

**Definition I.1 (Para; pretrained model).** [Def] For a cartesian category $\mathcal C$, the bicategory $\mathbf{Para}(\mathcal C)$ is defined as follows (Fong–Spivak–Tuyéras; Cruttwell–Gavranović–Ghani–Wilson–Zanasi):
- its 1-cells $X\to Y$ are pairs $(P,\,f:P\times X\to Y)$;
- its 2-cells $(P,f)\Rightarrow(Q,g)$ are maps $r:Q\to P$ with $g=f\circ(r\times X)$.

A **pretrained model** is a pointed 1-cell $F=(\Theta,\theta_0,f)$ of $\mathbf{Para}(\mathbf{Diff})$. Its realisation is $\Phi_f:\Theta\to C^\infty(X,Y)$, $\theta\mapsto f(\theta,-)$. Fine-tuning is a path $\theta(t)$ with $\theta(0)=\theta_0$.

**Definition I.2 (methods and simulations).** [Def] A weight-space **PEFT method** at $\theta_0$ is a pointed smooth map $M=(Q,q_0,\rho)$, $\rho:Q\to\Theta$, with $\rho(q_0)=\theta_0$. Usually $\dim Q\ll\dim\Theta$, and $\theta_0$ (or a splitting of it) is frozen inside $\rho$. These form the pointed slice
$$\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta,\theta_0):=\mathbf{Diff}_*/(\Theta,\theta_0).$$
- A **morphism** $h:M\to N$ is a pointed smooth map with $\rho_N\circ h=\rho_M$. Read it as "$N$ simulates $M$": $N$ reproduces every weight that $M$ reaches, together with its parameter path. A *weak* morphism drops the condition $h(q_0)=q_0'$.
- The **budget** is $\lvert M\rvert=\dim Q$.
- $M$ is **additive** if $\rho=\theta_0+\delta$ with $\delta(q_0)=0$ and $\delta$ independent of $\theta_0$.
- $M$ is of **action type** if $\rho(q)=\gamma(q)\cdot\theta_0$ for a map $\gamma$ into a group acting on $\Theta$.

**Remark I.3 (orientation, and what Para adds).** Each $\rho$ is a 2-cell $F\Rightarrow F^\rho:=(Q,f\circ(\rho\times X))$ *out of* $F$. A simulation $h:M\to N$ is a 2-cell $F^{\rho_N}\Rightarrow F^{\rho_M}$, since $\rho_N\circ h=\rho_M$. Hence $\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta,\theta_0)$ is the **opposite of the pointed coslice of reparametrisation 2-cells under $F$**. It is not the slice over $F$; that slice consists of extensions (Exposé VI).

$\mathsf{Meth}$ depends only on $(\Theta,\theta_0)$ and not on $f$. So for weight-space methods, Para is bookkeeping. The network enters in exactly three places, and we use Para only there:
- extensions and mergeability (Exposé VI);
- the realisation $\Phi_f$, through functional images and descent (Obs II.15);
- the backpropagation lens (Exposé IV).

**Observation I.4 (universal properties).** [Obs] In $\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta,\theta_0)$:

(a) the frozen model $(\mathrm{pt},*,\theta_0)$ is initial, and full fine-tuning $(\Theta,\theta_0,\mathrm{id})$ is terminal. The unique morphism $M\to$ Full FT is $\rho_M$ itself.

(b) If the fibre product $Q_M\times_\Theta Q_N$ exists in $\mathbf{Diff}$, it is the product $M\times N$. It exists, for instance, when the set-level fibre product is an embedded submanifold of $Q_M\times Q_N$: a clean intersection, or linear/affine $\rho$'s as in Prop I.8(a). Transversality $\rho_M\pitchfork\rho_N$ is never the reason when $\lvert M\rvert+\lvert N\rvert<\dim\Theta$, since at $(q_0,q_0')$ it would force $\lvert M\rvert+\lvert N\rvert\ge\dim\Theta$. In $\mathbf{Set}$ the fibre product always exists and $\mathrm{Im}(M\times N)=\mathrm{Im}\,M\cap\mathrm{Im}\,N$. Whenever the product exists in $\mathsf{Meth}$, its underlying set is this set-level fibre product, so the image identity holds in every case. The product can fail to exist: on $\Theta=\mathbb R$ ($1\times1$ matrices), $\mathrm{LoRA}_1\times\text{Frozen}$ would have the cross $\{BA=0\}\subset\mathbb R^2$ as underlying set, and no manifold structure on it represents smooth maps into the cross. Both axes would have to lift smoothly through the point over the origin, which forces rank $2$ there.

(c) $\mathrm{Im}$ is a functor to the poset of subsets of $\Theta$ containing $\theta_0$. Conversely, $\mathrm{Im}\,M\subseteq\mathrm{Im}\,N$ yields a morphism of the set-level slice $\mathbf{Set}_*/(\Theta,\theta_0)$, but not necessarily a smooth one. Take $\rho_M(t)=t$ and $\rho_N(u)=u^3$ on $\mathbb R$, pointed at $0$: the images agree, yet no smooth $h:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ satisfies $\rho_N\circ h=\mathrm{id}$, i.e. $h(t)^3=t$.

*Proof.* (a) The only pointed map $\mathrm{pt}\to Q$ is $*\mapsto q_0$, and it lies over $\Theta$ because $\rho(q_0)=\theta_0$. A morphism into the terminal object must satisfy $\mathrm{id}\circ h=\rho_M$.

(b) Products in a pointed slice are pullbacks in $\mathbf{Diff}_*$. The forgetful functor $\mathbf{Diff}_*\to\mathbf{Diff}$ creates limits, since $\mathbf{Diff}_*$ is the coslice $\mathrm{pt}/\mathbf{Diff}$, and the induced map out of a pointed test object is pointed because its components are. Smooth maps landing in an embedded submanifold are smooth into it, which gives the sufficient condition. Transversality at $(q_0,q_0')$ means $d\rho_M(T_{q_0})+d\rho_N(T_{q_0'})=T_{\theta_0}\Theta$, and the left side has dimension at most $\lvert M\rvert+\lvert N\rvert$. A point $\theta\in\mathrm{Im}M\cap\mathrm{Im}N$ has preimages $q,q'$, and $(q,q')$ lies in the fibre product. Testing a product in $\mathsf{Meth}$ against pointed two-point $0$-manifolds shows that its points are exactly the pairs $(q,q')$ over a common $\theta$. For $\mathrm{LoRA}_1\times\text{Frozen}$, suppose a product $P$ exists, with $\iota:P\to Q_{\mathrm{LoRA}_1}=\mathbb R^2$. The forgetful functor $\mathbf{Diff}_*\to\mathbf{Diff}$ is a right adjoint (its left adjoint is $X\mapsto X\sqcup\mathrm{pt}$), so $P$ is also the pullback in $\mathbf{Diff}$ and may be tested with unpointed curves. Let $p^*$ be the unique point of $P$ over $(0,0)$. The two axis curves $t\mapsto(t,0)$ and $t\mapsto(0,t)$ lie over $\theta_0$, so each lifts to a smooth curve in $P$ through $p^*$, and their velocities map under $d\iota_{p^*}$ to $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$. Hence $\operatorname{rank}d\iota_{p^*}=2$. Rank is lower semicontinuous and at most $2$, so it equals $2$ near $p^*$, and $\iota$ maps a neighbourhood of $p^*$ onto a set containing an open subset of $\mathbb R^2$. The cross contains none. The argument works for every pointing of $\mathrm{LoRA}_1$. Under the standard pointing $(0,a_0)$ with $a_0\neq0$, the base point is a smooth point of the cross and the obstruction sits at the origin.

(c) Functoriality is $\rho_N\circ h=\rho_M$. For the converse, choose (axiom of choice) for each $\theta\in\mathrm{Im}M$ a preimage $s(\theta)$ under $\rho_N$ with $s(\theta_0)=q_0'$, and put $h=s\circ\rho_M$. In the example the only candidate is the cube root, which is not differentiable at $0$. ∎

*What this explains.* Every *weight-space* method pointed at $\theta_0$ lies in the interval [frozen, full FT]; adapters, prefixes and ReFT are extensions (Exposé VI), not objects of $\mathsf{Meth}$. Defaults that are not no-ops at initialisation (HF FourierFT and VB-LoRA, `init_lora_weights=False`, LoRA-XS's small random $R$, methods on a quantised base) live over their own starting weight, or carry a pointing defect (Exposé V). "Merge into the base weights" is the canonical morphism to the terminal object. In exact arithmetic on a full-precision base this is definitional, since every object of $\mathsf{Meth}$ is mergeable by construction. Type-preserving merges into quantised bases are lossy (Prop V.2(d)), combining several adapters is a separate question (Prop V.4), and the remaining zero-overhead questions live on extensions (Exposé VI). What both $M$ and $N$ can express, $\mathrm{Im}\,M\cap\mathrm{Im}\,N$, always exists as a set. The categorical product, when it exists, is the universal method simulated by both, and its image is exactly this set. Prop I.8(a) computes a genuine one (LoRA-XS). Prop I.8(b) and Thm II.7 compute meets (intersections) in the poset of subsets of $\Theta$ containing $\theta_0$, not products in $\mathsf{Meth}$. The gap between $\mathbf{Set}$ and $\mathbf{Diff}$ in (c) is a smooth lifting obstruction. Obs I.6 detects its first-order instances: in the cube-root example, $S_1(M)=\mathbb R\not\subseteq S_1(N)=0$. Higher-order obstructions need finer invariants: $\rho_M(t)=t^3$ and $\rho_N(u)=u^5$ have equal images and $S_1=0$ on both sides, yet the only lift, $t^{3/5}$, is not $C^1$.

**Definition I.5 (invariants of an object).** [Def] For $M=(Q,q_0,\rho)$:
- the image $\mathrm{Im}\,M=\rho(Q)$;
- the **expressive dimension** $d(M)=\dim\mathrm{Im}\,M=\max_q\operatorname{rank}d\rho_q$;
- the **gauge waste** $g(M)=\lvert M\rvert-d(M)$;
- the **first-order image** $S_1(M)=d\rho_{q_0}(T_{q_0}Q)$, and the **first-order deficit** $d(M)-\dim S_1(M)$;
- the **gauge group** $\{\varphi\in\mathrm{Diff}(Q):\rho\circ\varphi=\rho\}$, which is unpointed, since a gauge transformation moves $q_0$ inside its fibre. In practice we exhibit a Lie group acting on $Q$ that preserves $\rho$ and is locally transitive on generic fibres; we call it the **generic gauge**.

The base point $\theta_0$ is **regular** for $M$ if the deficit is $0$, and an **apex** if the deficit is positive. We say **vertex** for the subset-level notion, the cone point of an image germ, and keep **apex** for the base-point notion. The two differ in general: at a crossing of two smooth branches the image is singular, yet the deficit can be $0$. In every case below with a positive deficit, the image germ is (diffeomorphic to) a cone with vertex $\theta_0$; the exception is DoRA, which inherits LoRA's deficit through its torus factor. A cone germ that is a $C^1$ manifold at its vertex equals its tangent space there, so it is linear. Hence the vertex of a non-linear cone is a singular point.

**Observation I.6 ($S_1$ is functorial).** [Obs] If $M\to N$ is a (pointed) morphism, then $S_1(M)\subseteq S_1(N)$. In particular, isomorphic methods have equal $S_1$ and equal first-order deficit. Monotonicity holds only along pointed morphisms: a weak morphism, such as a gauge map moving $q_0$ inside its fibre, can change $S_1$.

*Proof.* Apply the chain rule $d\rho_N\circ dh=d\rho_M$ at $q_0$, using $h(q_0)=q_0'$. If $h$ is an isomorphism, $dh_q$ is invertible for every $q$, so $\operatorname{rank}d\rho_M(q)=\operatorname{rank}d\rho_N(h(q))$. Hence $d(M)=d(N)$ (equivalently $\mathrm{Im}\,M=\mathrm{Im}\,N$), and since $dh_{q_0}$ is surjective, $S_1(M)=S_1(N)$. So the deficits agree. ∎

*What this explains.* $S_1$ is the set of weight-space directions available to the linearised (lazy, NTK) dynamics at initialisation: the lazy-regime kernel $J_f\,d\rho\,\Lambda\,d\rho^\top J_f^\top$ has range $J_f(S_1)$. That kernel also depends on $\Lambda$ and on the network Jacobian $J_f$, not only on $S_1$. For random-$A_0$ zero-init LoRA it approximates the full fine-tuning kernel (Malladi et al. 2023), so a large first-order deficit need not cost much on the data. $S_1$ is an isomorphism invariant that separates initialisations the image cannot: zero-init $\mathrm T_A$ against $\mathrm T_B$, and the choice of $\mathrm{row}\,A_0$ (Thm II.2). The image already separates split initialisations, since it determines $P$.

**Observation I.7 (additive monoidal structure).** [Obs] $\Theta$ is a vector space, so every object of $\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta,\theta_0)$ can be written $\rho=\theta_0+\delta$ with $\delta:=\rho-\theta_0$ and $\delta(q_0)=0$. Put
$$M\oplus N:=(Q_M\times Q_N,(q_0,q_0'),\theta_0+\delta_M+\delta_N).$$
This is a symmetric monoidal structure with the frozen model as unit. Its image is the Minkowski sum $\theta_0+(\mathrm{Im}\,\delta_M+\mathrm{Im}\,\delta_N)$. The operation $\oplus$ is not the categorical product, whose image, when the product exists, is $\cap$ (Obs I.4(b)). "Additive" in Def I.2 ($\delta$ independent of $\theta_0$) is a condition on *families* $\theta_0\mapsto M(\theta_0)$; it matters for base change (Exposé V), not here.

Write $\mathrm{LoRA}_k$ for LoRA with frozen residual $\theta_0$; for split pointings the sum below has residual $\theta_0-P-P'$. Concatenation $h(B_1,A_1,B_2,A_2)=([B_1\,B_2],[A_1;A_2])$ is an isomorphism $\mathrm{LoRA}_r\oplus\mathrm{LoRA}_s\cong\mathrm{LoRA}_{r+s}$ of unpointed objects. It is a pointed isomorphism onto $\mathrm{LoRA}_{r+s}$ pointed at $([B_0\,B_0'],[A_0;A_0'])$. For $\mathrm T_A$ pointings ($B_0=0$, $\operatorname{rank}A_0=r$), the target pointing has $\operatorname{rank}[A_0;A_0']=r+s$ iff $\mathrm{row}\,A_0\cap\mathrm{row}\,A_0'=0$; it lies in the stratum $\mathrm T_A$ of Thm II.2 when moreover $r+s<\min(m,n)$.

Standard pointings ($\mathrm T_A$, or symmetrically $\mathrm T_B$) exist only for $r<\min(m,n)$ (Thm II.2), so the monoidal question is pointwise. If $F(r)$ and $F(2r)$ are standard ($2r<\min(m,n)$), then $F(r)\oplus F(r)\not\cong F(2r)$ as pointed objects; for $2r\ge\min(m,n)$ no standard $F(2r)$ exists. Hence no assignment $r\mapsto\mathrm{LoRA}_r$ with $F(1)$ and $F(2)$ standard is **strong monoidal** into pointed objects. For $r\neq s$ and standard pointings, $F(r)\oplus F(s)\cong F(r+s)$ as pointed objects exactly when the row space of the target's $A_0$ is the direct sum of the two source row spaces. The assignment is strong monoidal on unpointed objects, up to weak morphisms, and on pointed objects with degenerate pointings: the origin $(B_0,A_0)=(0,0)$, where $S_1=0$, or the stacked pointings $F(r)=F(1)^{\oplus r}$. Concatenation is strictly associative and unital, so discrete $(\mathbb N,+)$ suffices for a plain strong monoidal functor. *Symmetric* strong monoidality needs the permutation groupoid, since swapping and then concatenating permutes the blocks.

With per-adapter scales $s_r$ ($\alpha/r$ for LoRA, $\alpha/\sqrt r$ for rsLoRA) and any target scale $s_*$, the map $h=([B_1\,B_2],[c_1A_1;c_2A_2])$ with $c_i=s_{r_i}/s_*$ is an isomorphism over $\Theta$. It is a linear bijection and preserves row spaces, so the pointing criterion is unchanged. HF PEFT's `add_weighted_adapter(..., combination_type="cat")` evaluates such a map at trained points: it sets `lora_alpha` to the new rank, so $s_*=1$, and multiplies each $A_i$ by $w_is_{r_i}$, so $c_i=w_is_{r_i}$. It is the isomorphism $h$ when all weights $w_i=1$ and the merged adapter has `use_rslora=False`. For rsLoRA sources, released PEFT versions through v0.21.1 copy `use_rslora=True` into the merged config and so overscale the merge by $\sqrt{r+s}$; PR #3449 on main fixes this.

*Proof.* The coherence isomorphisms of $\oplus$ are those of the cartesian structure on $\mathbf{Diff}_*$. They lie over $\Theta$ because $+$ is associative and commutative, and the frozen model is a unit because its $\delta$ is $0$. Next, $[B_1\,B_2][A_1;A_2]=B_1A_1+B_2A_2$, and $h$ is a linear bijection. The pointing claim is $\dim(U+V)=r+s-\dim(U\cap V)$ for $U=\mathrm{row}A_0$, $V=\mathrm{row}A_0'$. For the negative claim, any pointed isomorphism preserves $S_1$ (Obs I.6). If $F(r)$ is standard, $S_1(F(r)\oplus F(r))=\{XA_0+X'A_0\}$ has dimension $mr$, while a standard $F(2r)$ has $\dim S_1=2mr$. For $r\neq s$, $S_1(F(r)\oplus F(s))=\{Y[A_0;A_0']\}$ equals $S_1(F(r+s))=\{XA_0''\}$ iff $\mathrm{row}A_0''=\mathrm{row}A_0\oplus\mathrm{row}A_0'$; then concatenation followed by the gauge $g\in GL_{r+s}$ with $g[A_0;A_0']=A_0''$ is a pointed isomorphism. The degenerate cases are direct. ∎ [Num: at $m=7$, $\dim S_1=28$ for a standard $\mathrm{LoRA}_4$, against $14$ for $\mathrm{LoRA}_2\oplus\mathrm{LoRA}_2$ with the same $A_0$ and $28$ with independent $A_0$'s; $F(1)\oplus F(2)$ and a standard $F(3)$ both give $21$.]

*What this explains.* RoSA (LoRA $\oplus$ a fixed-mask sparse method) and GraLoRA ($\oplus$ of $k^2$ block-embedded LoRAs) are $\oplus$-words, and their expressivity is a Minkowski sum. ReLoRA is $\mathrm{LoRA}^{\oplus K}$ unrolled in time, ignoring its full-rank warm start. HF's `cat` merge is the isomorphism $h$ evaluated at trained points; the merged adapter, if unfrozen, is $\mathrm{LoRA}_{r+s}$. A sum of $k$ rank-$r$ LoRAs, each with residual $\theta_0$, is isomorphic in $\mathsf{Meth}$ to one LoRA of rank $kr$, pointed at the concatenated initialisation. Under $\alpha/r$ scaling the isomorphism rescales the $A$-blocks, so training at equal $\alpha$ and learning rate follows a different trajectory. LoRA-soup CAT freezes the trained modules and learns layer-wise weights, so it is a $\oplus$ of $k$ frozen line methods $\alpha_i\mapsto\alpha_iB_iA_i$. Its per-layer image is a span of dimension at most $k$ (generically exactly $k$), not $\mathrm{LoRA}_{kr}$. UniPELT and MAM are **not** $\oplus$-words in $\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta,\theta_0)$: their adapter and prefix parts live on extensions (Exposé VI), and UniPELT's gates depend on the input. With a fixed pointing, stacking the same $A_0$ twice gives a rank-deficient $A_0$, which $S_1$ distinguishes from a standard $\mathrm{LoRA}_{2r}$.

**Proposition I.8 (a product that produces a named method; an image-level meet).** [Prop]

(a) Let $A_0\in\mathbb R^{r\times n}$ have full row rank and $B_0\in\mathbb R^{m\times r}$ full column rank. Define the one-sided methods
$$\mathrm{FA}(A_0):B\mapsto W_0+BA_0,\qquad\mathrm{FB}(B_0):A\mapsto W_0+B_0A,$$
both pointed at $0$. Then
$$\mathrm{FA}(A_0)\times\mathrm{FB}(B_0)\;\cong\;(\mathbb R^{r\times r},0,\ R\mapsto W_0+B_0RA_0).$$
The product supplies this sandwich for *any* frames. Assume $\operatorname{rank}W_0\ge r$ ($\sigma_r>0$) and take the SVD frames $B_0=U_r$, $A_0=\Sigma_rV_r^\top$: the result is **LoRA-XS pointed at $R=0$**. The LoRA-XS paper writes $xW+xARB$ in row-vector convention with $A=U_r\Sigma_r$ and $B=V_r^\top$; transposed, the update is $B_0R^\top A_0$, so the isomorphism is $R\mapsto\alpha R^\top$. LoRA-XS initialises $R\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2)$ with $\sigma=10^{-5}$, a negligible pointing defect.

(b) Let $W_0$ have full row rank (so $m\le n$), and let $\mathrm{LoRA}_r$ carry its standard pointing ($B_0=0$, image $W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}$). The image-level meet of $\mathrm{LoRA}_r$ with the output-scaling method $\ell\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(\ell)W_0$ is $\{\mathrm{diag}(\ell)W_0:\#\{i:\ell_i\neq1\}\le r\}$, that is, "rescale at most $r$ output neurons". The output-scaling method is the key/value part of (IA)$^3$. On the FFN, (IA)$^3$ scales the *input* of $W_{\rm down}$, $W\mapsto W\mathrm{diag}(\ell)$; the mirror statement then needs full column rank, or holds generically for $r<d$.

*Proof.* (a) A point of the fibre product is a pair $(B,A)$ with $BA_0=B_0A=:D$. Then $\mathrm{row}\,D\subseteq\mathrm{row}\,A_0$ and $\mathrm{col}\,D\subseteq\mathrm{col}\,B_0$, so $D=B_0RA_0$ for a unique $R$. Since $A_0$ has full row rank, $BA_0=B_0RA_0$ forces $B=B_0R$, and likewise $A=RA_0$. So the fibre product is the $r^2$-dimensional linear subspace $\{(B_0R,RA_0)\}$, and $R\mapsto(B_0R,RA_0)$ is a smooth bijection onto it with smooth inverse $(B,A)\mapsto B_0^+B$. Any pair of maps $(h_1,h_2):T\to Q_{\rm FA}\times Q_{\rm FB}$ that agree over $\Theta$ factors through it via $t\mapsto B_0^+h_1(t)$, so this is the product in $\mathbf{Diff}$. If $\operatorname{rank}W_0<r$, then $\Sigma_rV_r^\top$ is rank-deficient and the LoRA-XS instance breaks.

(b) $\mathrm{diag}(\ell)W_0-W_0=\mathrm{diag}(\ell-\mathbf 1)W_0$, and its rank is $\#\{i:\ell_i\ne1\}$ because the rows of $W_0$ are independent. *No product in (b) for $n\ge2$.* The set-level fibre product is $\mathcal F=\{(B,A,\ell):W_0+sBA=\mathrm{diag}(\ell)W_0\}$, whatever the pointing of LoRA$_r$. If a product $P$ existed, then, as in the proof of Obs I.4(b), its canonical map $\iota$ would be a bijection onto $\mathcal F$, $P$ would also be the pullback in $\mathbf{Diff}$, and smooth curves in $\mathcal F$ would lift to $P$. For every $B'$ and $A'$ the curves $t\mapsto(tB',0,\mathbf 1)$ and $t\mapsto(0,tA',\mathbf 1)$ lie in $\mathcal F$ and lift through the point $p^*$ over $(0,0,\mathbf 1)$, so the projection of $\iota$ to the LoRA parameters has surjective differential at $p^*$; it is then a submersion near $p^*$, and its image contains a nonempty open set of pairs $(B,A)$. But every pair in that image has $BA=\mathrm{diag}(\ell-\mathbf 1)W_0/s$, so the first row of $BA$ is parallel to the first row of $W_0$, which is nonzero. For $n\ge2$ one of the $2\times2$ minors expressing this is a polynomial in $(B,A)$ that is not identically zero, and its zero set has empty interior, a contradiction. For $m=n=1$ the fibre product is the graph of $\ell=1+sBA/W_0$, and the product exists. ∎ [Num: the fibre product of (a) has dimension $4=r^2$ at $(m,n,r)=(6,8,2)$.]

*What this explains.* LoRA-XS is the largest method that factors through *both* frozen frames. In the frames of an initialisation it is the overlap that a split initialisation double-counts (Prop II.3); it is LoRA-XS proper when those frames span the top-$r$ singular subspaces of $W_0$, as at a PiSSA init. The meet in (b) is only set-level. It is the union of the $\binom mr$ coordinate $r$-planes "rescale at most $r$ output neurons", and for $n\ge2$ the product does not exist in $\mathbf{Diff}$, because its fibre product is singular at the origin $(b,a,\ell)=(0,0,\mathbf 1)$, by the rank argument of Obs I.4(b) (see the proof of Prop I.8; for $m=n=1$ the product exists). For $r=1$ it is the union of the linear spaces $\{b=0,\ell=\mathbf 1\}$ and $\{a=0,\ell=\mathbf 1\}$ with $m$ two-dimensional sheets $\{b=ce_i,\ a=\lambda w_i,\ \ell_i=1+sc\lambda\}$, where $w_i$ is row $i$ of $W_0$. Under the standard pointing ($b=0$, $a_0$ generic) the base point is a smooth point of $\{b=0,\ell=\mathbf 1\}$. Once a support is fixed it is just (IA)$^3$ masked to $r$ neurons; compare sparse activation scaling (Stoehr et al. 2024).

---

## Exposé II. The image germ: expressivity

### II.A Initialisations: pointings and strata

**Theorem II.1 (pointings of an additive method).** [Thm; elementary. The rank-$2r$ consequence is known in practice as the PiSSA$\to$LoRA conversion (Meng et al. 2024), implemented in HF PEFT] Let $M=(Q,q_0,\theta_0+\delta)$ be additive with shape $C=\delta(Q)$. Write $-v+C$ for a translate, and $C-C:=\{c-c':c,c'\in C\}$ for the Minkowski difference.

(a) For each $q\in Q$, the re-pointed method $M_q:=(Q,q,\theta_0-\delta(q)+\delta(\cdot))$, with frozen residual $\theta_0-\delta(q)$, is pointed at $\theta_0$, and $\mathrm{Im}\,M_q=\theta_0+(-\delta(q)+C)$.

(b) $\bigcup_q\mathrm{Im}\,M_q=\theta_0+(C-C)$.

(c) For LoRA$_r$ on a single linear box with $s\ne0$, $C-C=\mathcal M_{\le\min(2r,m,n)}$; for several boxes, $C-C$ is the product of the per-box varieties $\mathcal M_{\le\min(2r,m_i,n_i)}$. Hence **no exact pointing of LoRA$_r$ (frozen residual $\theta_0-\delta(q)$), whether zero-init or split (PiSSA, OLoRA, CorDA, LoRA-GA), makes an update $\theta-\theta_0$ of rank $>2r$ reachable in any adapted matrix.** More robustly, from *any* starting point $q_0$ of LoRA$_r$, pointed or not, $\rho(q)-\rho(q_0)=\delta(q)-\delta(q_0)$ has rank $\le\min(2r,m,n)$. Quantised-residual schemes (QLoRA, LoftQ, QPiSSA) are pointed only up to an order-dependent defect ($e$ for QLoRA; see Prop V.2(b) for the others); measured from $\theta_0$ rather than from their starting weight, their updates contain this defect, whose rank is generically at least $\min(m,n)-r$. The bound $2r$ is attained by split pointings when $2r\le\min(m,n)$.

*Proof.* (a) and (b) are immediate from the definitions. (c) $\operatorname{rank}(X-Y)\le 2r$. Conversely, $C$ is a cone and so closed under negation; split the SVD of a rank-$\le 2r$ matrix as $Z=Z_1+Z_2$ with $\operatorname{rank}Z_i\le r$, and write $Z=Z_1-(-Z_2)$. ∎

*What this explains.* An initialisation of an additive method chooses which *translate* of the shape passes through $\theta_0$. Splitting initialisations (PiSSA-type) buy "spectral replacement" updates $Y-P$, which can have rank $2r$. They pay by losing generic updates of every rank $\ge1$ (Thm II.2(d)).

**Theorem II.2 (three strata of LoRA initialisations).** [Thm] Let $\delta(B,A)=BA$ with $1\le r<\min(m,n)$. Consider pointed LoRA objects in three distinguished strata of full-rank pointings (pointings whose nonzero factors have full rank). Only $\mathrm S$ is open and dense in the space of pointings; $\mathrm T_A$ and $\mathrm T_B$ are nowhere dense.
- $(\mathrm T_A)$: $B_0=0$ and $\operatorname{rank}A_0=r$;
- $(\mathrm T_B)$: $A_0=0$ and $\operatorname{rank}B_0=r$;
- $(\mathrm S)$: $\operatorname{rank}B_0A_0=r$, with frozen residual $\theta_0-B_0A_0$.

(a) **Classification.** Within a stratum, $M_{q_0}\cong M_{q_0'}$ iff, respectively, $\mathrm{row}\,A_0=\mathrm{row}\,A_0'$; $\mathrm{col}\,B_0=\mathrm{col}\,B_0'$; or $B_0A_0=B_0'A_0'$. Objects in different strata are never isomorphic. As a *set*, the isomorphism classes of the three strata therefore form
$$\mathrm{Gr}(r,\mathbb R^n)\ \sqcup\ \mathrm{Gr}(r,\mathbb R^m)\ \sqcup\ \mathcal M_r(m\times n).$$
(b) **First order.** $\dim S_1$ is $mr$, $nr$ and $r(m+n-r)$ respectively, while $d=r(m+n-r)$ in every case. The deficits are $r(n-r)$, $r(m-r)$ and $0$.

(c) **Geometry.** In $\mathrm T_A$ and $\mathrm T_B$, $\mathrm{Im}=\theta_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}$, whose vertex $\theta_0$ is an **apex**. In $\mathrm S$, $\mathrm{Im}=(\theta_0-P)+\mathcal M_{\le r}$ with $P=B_0A_0$, and $\theta_0$ is a **smooth point**.

(d) **Incomparability.** The images of $\mathrm S$-type and $\mathrm T$-type objects are incomparable.

(e) **Scope.** The three strata do not exhaust the pointings of LoRA$_r$. Outside them lie: $B_0=0$ with $\operatorname{rank}A_0<r$; $A_0=0$ with $\operatorname{rank}B_0<r$; $B_0=A_0=0$; $0<\operatorname{rank}B_0A_0<r$; and pointings with $B_0,A_0$ both nonzero and $B_0A_0=0$. HF PEFT ships one of the last kind: `init_lora_weights="orthogonal"` (which requires $r$ even) lies in the stratum $(\operatorname{rk}B_0,\operatorname{rk}A_0,\operatorname{rk}B_0A_0)=(r/2,r/2,0)$, an apex with $\dim S_1=\tfrac r2(m+n-\tfrac r2)$. It is isomorphic to no object of the three strata. Its image $\theta_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}$ rules out $\mathrm S$, and its $S_1$ determines $(\mathrm{col}\,B_0,\mathrm{row}\,A_0)$, of dimensions $(r/2,r/2)$, against $(0,r)$ in $\mathrm T_A$ and $(r,0)$ in $\mathrm T_B$. The dimension of $S_1$ alone does not separate it, since at $(m,n,r)=(4,5,2)$ it equals $mr$. This placement holds in exact arithmetic; HF casts the two factors to the base dtype separately, and in bf16 the result is a generic pointing with a tiny defect. For every pointing, the image determines $P=B_0A_0$ (its vertex is $\theta_0-P$), and $S_1=\{XA_0+B_0Y\}$ determines $(\mathrm{row}\,A_0,\mathrm{col}\,B_0)$ whenever these are proper subspaces. The classification is set-level: in the space of pointings $\mathrm T_A$ lies in the closure of $\mathrm S$, since $(\varepsilon B',A_0)\to(0,A_0)$, so the quotient is not a topological disjoint union.

*Proof.*
- *"If" in (a).* Two full-row-rank matrices with the same row space satisfy $A_0'=gA_0$ for a unique $g\in GL_r$, and $(B,A)\mapsto(Bg^{-1},gA)$ is a pointed isomorphism over $\Theta$. $\mathrm T_B$ is symmetric. In $\mathrm S$, the full-rank factorisations of $P$ form one $GL_r$-orbit, and the residuals agree.
- *"Only if" in (a).* In $\mathrm T_A$, $d\rho_{(0,A_0)}(\dot B,\dot A)=\dot BA_0$. So $S_1=\{XA_0\}$ is the set of matrices whose row space lies in $\mathrm{row}A_0$; it determines $\mathrm{row}A_0$, and it is an isomorphism invariant by Obs I.6. $\mathrm T_B$ is symmetric. In $\mathrm S$, the image $(\theta_0-P)+\mathcal M_{\le r}$ is an isomorphism invariant and determines its vertex: $\mathrm{Sing}\,\mathcal M_{\le s}=\mathcal M_{\le s-1}$ for $1\le s\le r<\min(m,n)$, algebraically and in the $C^1$ sense (a non-linear cone is not $C^1$ at its vertex), so iterating the singular locus $r$ times leaves the single point $\theta_0-P$. Hence the image determines $P$. A shorter route avoids singular loci: if $c+\mathcal M_{\le r}=c'+\mathcal M_{\le r}$ with $D=c-c'\neq0$, a generic $X$ of rank $r$ gives $\operatorname{rank}(D+X)=\min(\operatorname{rk}D+r,m,n)\ge r+1$, so $c+X\notin c'+\mathcal M_{\le r}$.
- *Separating the strata.* $S_1(\mathrm T_A)=\{Z:\mathrm{row}Z\subseteq V\}$ contains $e_iv^\top$ for every $i$ and every $v\in V$, so it equals $S_1(\mathrm T_B)=\{Z:\mathrm{col}Z\subseteq U\}$ only if $U=\mathbb R^m$, which is impossible since $r<m$. (When $m\ne n$ the dimensions $mr\ne nr$ already differ.) $\mathrm S$ differs from both because $\dim S_1=r(m+n-r)$ differs from $mr$ (as $n>r$) and from $nr$ (as $m>r$).
- *(b).* At a full-rank pair, $S_1=\{XA_0+B_0Y\}=T_P\mathcal M_r$. In general $\dim\{XA_0+B_0Y\}=m\operatorname{rk}A_0+n\operatorname{rk}B_0-\operatorname{rk}A_0\operatorname{rk}B_0$ (Prop II.3).
- *(c).* $\mathcal M_r$ is open in $\mathcal M_{\le r}$ and is a manifold of dimension $r(m+n-r)$, so $P$, of rank $r$, is a smooth point, and $\theta_0$ is a smooth point of the $\mathrm S$-image. In the $\mathrm T$ strata, $\theta_0$ is the vertex of the non-linear cone $\theta_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}$, a singular point (Def I.5), and by (b) its deficit is positive, so it is an apex.
- *(d).* A generic rank-one $X$ satisfies $\operatorname{rank}(P+X)=\operatorname{rank}(X-P)=r+1$. So $\theta_0+X$ lies in the $\mathrm T$-image but not in the $\mathrm S$-image, and $\theta_0-P+X$ lies in the $\mathrm S$-image but not in the $\mathrm T$-image.
- *(e).* The dimension is Prop II.3. For every pointing, $S_1=\{XA_0+B_0Y\}=\{Z:Z(\mathrm{row}\,A_0)^\perp\subseteq\mathrm{col}\,B_0\}$, whose Frobenius annihilator is $(\mathrm{col}\,B_0)^\perp\otimes(\mathrm{row}\,A_0)^\perp$. When both subspaces are proper, the column and row spans of this annihilator recover them, so $S_1$ determines the pair and its dimensions, and by Obs I.6 so does every pointed isomorphism class. The image determines its vertex $\theta_0-P$ by the translation argument above. HF's `orthogonal` init draws one orthogonal $Q\in O(r)$ and sets $A_0=(G_AQ_{[0::2]})^\top/10$, $B_0=G_BQ_{[1::2]}/10$, from the even- and odd-indexed rows of $Q$, so $B_0A_0=0$ with both factors of rank $r/2$. ∎ [Num: $\dim S_1=(14,10,20)$ for $(\mathrm T_A,\mathrm T_B,\mathrm S)$ at $(m,n,r)=(7,5,2)$, and $11$ for HF's `orthogonal` init.]

*What this explains.* Most LoRA initialisation schemes in the literature are **points of these three strata**; the table also lists the exceptions.

| scheme | stratum | point |
|---|---|---|
| vanilla LoRA (Linear, Conv) | $\mathrm T_A$ | random point of $\mathrm{Gr}(r,n)$: Haar under Gaussian $A_0$, only approximately Haar under Kaiming-uniform |
| vanilla LoRA on `nn.Embedding` (loralib and HF default) | $\mathrm T_B$ | $A_0=0$, $B_0\sim\mathcal N(0,1)$ in the convention $y=Wx$ with $m$ the embedding dimension; deficit $r(m-r)$ |
| EVA | $\mathrm T_A$ | top-$r$ principal subspace of the input activations, at its per-layer rank |
| LoRA-FA | sub-object of $\mathrm T_A$ | the same point with $A$ frozen (an arrow $\mathrm{FA}(A_0)\to\mathrm{LoRA}_{(0,A_0)}$) |
| "Init[B]" (Hayou et al. 2024) | $\mathrm T_B$ | random point of $\mathrm{Gr}(r,m)$ |
| MiCA | sub-object of $\mathrm T_B$ | $B_0$ = minor left-singular frame, frozen: $\mathrm{FB}(B_0)\to\mathrm{LoRA}_{(B_0,0)}$, a linear image at a regular point (HF also allows $r=\min(m,n)$, outside the theorem) |
| PiSSA / MiLoRA | $\mathrm S$ | $P$ = top / bottom rank-$r$ SVD part of $W_0$ (HF absorbs the scale: $P=sB_0A_0$) |
| OLoRA | $\mathrm S$ | $P=sQ_rR_r$ in HF, from a truncated QR factorisation |
| CorDA | $\mathrm S$ | $P$ from the SVD of $W_0C$, where $C$ is the activation covariance |
| LoRA-GA | $\mathrm S$ | $P$ from the SVD of the first gradient |
| Astra | $\mathrm S$ | $P=sVV^\top W_0$ with $V$ eigenvectors of the output activations (paper: centred covariance, tail eigenvectors; HF and the official code: uncentred second moment, top $r$, scale not absorbed) |
| HF `orthogonal` | outside: $(r/2,r/2,0)$ | an apex with $\dim S_1=\tfrac r2(m+n-\tfrac r2)$, in exact arithmetic |
| QLoRA, LoftQ | defect, not a point | zero-init (QLoRA) or approximately split (LoftQ) over a quantised base (Prop V.2) |
| HF `init_lora_weights=False` | unpointed | random $A$ and $B$, with no subtraction |

Zero-init LoRA starts at a *singular point*. At first order the $A$-direction is dead, so LoRA and LoRA-FA (which freezes $A$ and trains $B$; Zhang et al. use AdamW) have the same $K_{q_0}$ under plain gradient steps and the same first-order trajectory under Adam (Lemma II.4). Splitting initialisations start at a smooth point with full first-order reach. **[Conj]** This is one mechanism behind the faster early progress reported for PiSSA and LoRA-GA. LoRA-GA attributes its speed-up to gradient alignment, and MiLoRA also starts at a smooth point, so the smooth start alone does not account for the reported gains. The strata classify *expressivity together with $S_1$*. What an optimizer additionally sees is the subject of Cor III.13.

**Proposition II.3 (first-order rank formula: smoothness, alignment and scale).** [Prop] For $\kappa(B,A)=W_{\rm base}+sBA$ with $s\ne0$,
$$\operatorname{rank}d\kappa_{(B,A)}=m\operatorname{rk}A+n\operatorname{rk}B-\operatorname{rk}A\operatorname{rk}B\qquad\text{for all }(B,A),$$
and $\{XA\}\cap\{BY\}=\{BRA:R\in\mathbb R^{r\times r}\}$. For full-rank frames this overlap is the tangent space of the product $\mathrm{FA}(A)\times\mathrm{FB}(B)$ of Prop I.8(a), re-based at $\kappa(B,A)$, whose image is the meet of the two one-sided images: a generalised LoRA-XS. It is LoRA-XS proper when $(B,A)$ span the top-$r$ singular subspaces of $W_0$ ($\sigma_r>0$), as at a PiSSA init; bottom frames (MiLoRA) give a generalised LoRA-XS.

Under gradient flow $\dot B=-\eta_B\nabla_BL$, $\dot A=-\eta_A\nabla_AL$, with $G=\nabla_WL$,
$$\tfrac{d}{dt}L=-s^2\big(\eta_B\|GA^\top\|_F^2+\eta_A\|B^\top G\|_F^2\big)$$
at every time, in particular at $(B_0,A_0)$. To first order in the step sizes the same holds for full-batch gradient descent, and in expectation for unbiased minibatch SGD. Under Adam ($\varepsilon\to0$, with bias correction) the first step changes the loss by $-\lvert s\rvert\big(\eta_B\|GA_0^\top\|_1+\eta_A\|B_0^\top G\|_1\big)+O(\eta^2)$, with entrywise $\ell_1$ norms. This first-order term is linear in $\lvert s\rvert$, against quadratic under gradient flow.

*Proof.* The image of $d\kappa$ is $\{XA\}+\{BY\}$, whose summands are the matrices with row space in $\mathrm{row}A$ (dimension $m\operatorname{rk}A$) and with column space in $\mathrm{col}B$ (dimension $n\operatorname{rk}B$). A matrix $D$ lies in both iff $D\in\mathrm{col}B\otimes\mathrm{row}A$, of dimension $\operatorname{rk}A\operatorname{rk}B$, and then $D=B(B^+DA^+)A$. The gradient-flow rate is $\langle G,\dot W\rangle$ with $\dot W=-s^2(\eta_BGA^\top A+\eta_ABB^\top G)$. Adam's first step is $\delta B=-\eta_B\,\mathrm{sign}(sGA^\top)$, so $\langle G,s\,\delta B\,A\rangle=-\lvert s\rvert\eta_B\|GA^\top\|_1$, and likewise for $A$. ∎ [Num: the rank formula holds in 400 random cases including rank-deficient factors; the rate matches finite differences.]

*What this explains.* Zero-factor initialisations (LoRA, QLoRA, EVA, LoRA-FA) have $mr$ first-order directions; split initialisations have $r(m+n-r)$. At $4096^2$ with $r=16$ this is 65,536 against 130,816. "Better initialisation" in the literature mixes three distinct mechanisms:
- **smoothness**: PiSSA and LoRA-GA start off the apex;
- **alignment**: under gradient flow, among $A_0$ with $A_0A_0^\top=cI$, the initial rate is maximised by the top-$r$ right singular space of $G$. LoRA-GA takes exactly this for $A_0$, but takes $B_0$ from the left singular vectors $r+1,\dots,2r$ (paper Alg. 1; HF's default `direction="ArB2r"`). $G$ then has no component in the overlap $\{B_0RA_0\}$, so the first gradient step approximates the rank-$2r$ truncation of $G$. LoRA-GA optimises gradient approximation, and gives up part of the $B$-term of the rate to do so. EVA uses the top principal subspace of the input activations. Its Thm 3.2 assumes the inputs independent of the upstream errors and bounds the expected per-sample squared gradient norm with the uncentred $\mathbb E[xx^\top]$. That quantity is not the rate $\|\mathbb E[G]A_0^\top\|^2$, and HF's EVA uses mean-centred PCA, so EVA's frame is a data-only proxy. Alignment is a gradient-flow and SGD notion: under Adam the first-step rate $\|GA_0^\top\|_1$ depends on the basis of $\mathrm{row}\,A_0$, not only on the subspace;
- **scale**: $\|GA_0^\top\|_F^2$ grows with $\|A_0\|^2$, and initial scale trades off against learning rate (Thm III.10). PiSSA's $S_r^{1/2}$-weighted factors change it. LoRA-GA's paper scales both factors by $d_{\rm out}^{1/4}/\sqrt\gamma$; HF's default `scale="stable"` scales only $B$ (which also carries $S_{r+1:2r}$), so there $\|GA_0^\top\|_F^2$ is unchanged. HF's Kaiming default has $A_0A_0^\top\approx I/3$ against $A_0A_0^\top=I$ for EVA with `whiten=False`. Comparing rates across initialisations needs $A_0A_0^\top$ normalised.

LoRA-GA uses all three mechanisms, with alignment on the $A$ side only.

**Lemma II.4 (zero gate).** [Lemma] Let $Q=U\times V$ with $U$ a vector space, let $\rho=\theta_0+\beta(u,v)$ with $\beta$ smooth, and suppose $\beta(u_0,v)=0$ for all $v$; for example $\beta(\cdot,v)$ linear and $u_0=0$. Then $D_v\rho_{q_0}=0$. Hence $\nabla_v(L\circ\rho)(q_0)=0$, $S_1=D_u\beta(u_0,v_0)(U)$ ($=\beta(U,v_0)$ in the linear case), and the cometric at $q_0$ is $K_{q_0}=D_u\beta\,\Lambda_{uu}\,D_u\beta^\top$, where $\Lambda_{uu}$ is the $uu$-block of $\Lambda$; no block-diagonality is needed, since the $v$-columns of $D\rho$ vanish. The weight trajectory $\theta(t)$ agrees with that of the method with $v$ frozen at $v_0$ to first order in $t$ (gradient flow), or in the step size (one or more Adam steps). The parameter $v$ itself may still move: under terms not of the form $L\circ\rho$ (regularisers, decoupled weight decay), and under Adam, whose step-2 move of $v$ is $O(\eta)$ although its effect on $\theta$ is $O(\eta^2)$.

*Function-level variant.* For an extension with realised function $F(u,v,x)$, if $F(u_0,v,x)=F_0(x)$ for all $v$ and $x$, then $D_vF=0$ at $q_0$, with the same conclusions at the level of functions. No linearity is needed.

*Proof.* $\beta(u_0,v)=0$ for all $v$, so $D_v\beta(u_0,v_0)=0$. The rest follows from the chain rule. The function-level variant is identical. ∎ [Num: under Adam with $\eta\in\{10^{-2},10^{-3},10^{-4}\}$, the step-2 move of $A$ in zero-init LoRA scales as $\eta$, while the deviation of $\Delta W$ from the frozen-$A$ method scales as $\eta^2$.]

*Instances.*
- LoRA: $u=B$.
- VeRA ($u=b$): $\theta$ first moves only along $b$.
- AdaLoRA ($u=\Lambda$, $\Lambda_0=0$): $\theta$ initially moves only along the $r$ rank-one directions $p_iq_i^\top$, so $\dim S_1=r$ for generic $P,Q$. $P$ and $Q$ do move at step one, through AdaLoRA's orthogonality penalty $R(P,Q)$ (HF default `orth_reg_weight=0.5`), which has no first-order effect on $\theta$.
- LLaMA-Adapter (function-level variant): only the gate moves at first order.
- Adapters with a zero up-projection (function-level variant), and LoHa with a zero factor (HF zero-initialises `hada_w2_b`): only that factor moves.

These first-step statements assume no weight decay on $v$. HRA's paired initialisation is not of this form, but it shows the same first-order collapse (Thm II.7(b)).

### II.B Erlangen: invariants of group-type methods

**Theorem II.5 (complete invariants of the basic actions).** [Thm; classical] Let $W,W'\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}$.
1. *Right orthogonal (input side).* $W'=WR$ for some $R\in O(n)$ iff $WW^\top=W'W'^\top$. For $SO(n)$, when $\operatorname{rank}W=n$, also require that the then unique $R$ have $\det R=1$.
2. *Left orthogonal.* $W'=PW$ for some $P\in O(m)$ iff $W^\top W=W'^\top W'$.
3. *Two-sided.* $W'=PWR$ for some $P\in O(m)$ and $R\in O(n)$ iff $W$ and $W'$ have the same singular values, counted with multiplicity.
4. *Left torus.* If $W$ has no zero row, $W'=DW$ for some $D\in T_m^+$ iff $w_i'\in\mathbb R_{>0}w_i$ for every $i$. For the full torus $T_m$, whose signs are unconstrained, the complete invariant is the set of zero rows together with the row *lines*.
5. *Full Hadamard torus.* $W'=E\odot W$ for some $E\in(\mathbb R^\times)^{m\times n}$ iff $W$ and $W'$ have the same zero pattern.

*Proof.* (1) If all inner products of rows agree, the map $\sum c_iw_i\mapsto\sum c_iw_i'$ is well defined and isometric on the row spans, because $\|\sum c_iw_i\|^2=c^\top WW^\top c$. The two row spans have the same dimension, $\operatorname{rank}WW^\top$. Extend the map by an isometry of the orthogonal complements to $R^\top\in O(n)$; then $W'=WR$. The converse is immediate. (2) is (1) applied to transposes. (3) follows from the SVD. (4) and (5) hold entry by entry; for (5) put $E_{ij}=W'_{ij}/W_{ij}$ on the support and $1$ off it. ∎

(1) is the orbit-separation consequence of the first fundamental theorem of invariant theory for $O(n)$ (for a compact group, invariants separate orbits); equivalently, it is Witt-type extension of isometries. The two-sided diagonal torus $\{\mathrm{diag}(a)\,X\,\mathrm{diag}(b)\}\cong(T_m\times T_n)/\mathbb R^\times$ that appears as LoHa's gauge (Prop II.12) is a different group from the full Hadamard torus in (5).

*Caveat for methods.* No PEFT method realises (4) or (5) as a group action on all of its parameters. (IA)$^3$ entries and DoRA magnitudes are unconstrained reals initialised at $1$ or at the row norms, so the relevant group in (4) is $T_m$ or its closure. HiRA's factor $J+BA$ can vanish entrywise. These methods can create new zeros, so for them only a one-way statement transfers: zeros of $W_0$ remain zero.

**Corollary II.6 (what exactly orthogonal methods can never do).** [Cor] Let a method have image in $W_0\cdot O(n)$ (input side), and work in exact arithmetic. This covers:
- OFT and block OFT with an exactly orthogonal parametrisation: the original paper's exact Cayley map, and HF PEFT from 0.16 with `use_cayley_neumann=False` on Linear and Conv targets;
- GOFT with strict Givens rotations and no scaler, as in the paper (`no_scaling=True` in the official peft-givens code);
- HRA, and ETHER as defined in its paper (not ETHER+);
- any number of merge-and-restart cycles of these.

Then:
- it preserves $K_{\rm out}$, hence every neuron norm and the angle between any two nonzero neurons, and so the hyperspherical energy, which depends only on these angles;
- it preserves the whole singular spectrum of $W_0$;
- block-diagonal OFT with input blocks $J$, with the same partition in every cycle, preserves each partial Gram matrix $W_{:,J}W_{:,J}^\top$.

*Remarks; these do not follow from the hypothesis.*
- *BOFT* as implemented in HF PEFT, $W=\mathrm{diag}(s)W_0R^\top$ with $R$ exactly orthogonal, has $K_{\rm out}(W)=\mathrm{diag}(s)K_{\rm out}(W_0)\mathrm{diag}(s)$. So $\cos'_{ij}=\mathrm{sign}(s_is_j)\cos_{ij}$ whenever $s_is_j\neq0$. It preserves $\lvert\cos\rvert$ when every $s_i$ is nonzero, and preserves $\cos_{ij}$ whenever $s_is_j>0$. Hence it preserves every cosine when all $s_i$ are nonzero and share a sign (for instance $s\in T_m^+$, the constraint in the re-scaled OFT of Qiu et al. 2023, §3.5); when no cosine of $W_0$ is zero, that condition is also necessary. HF's `boft_s` is an unconstrained real initialised at $1$. At the default `boft_n_butterfly_factor=1` the butterfly permutation is the identity, BOFT is block OFT times an output scale, and each input block satisfies $W_{:,J}W_{:,J}^\top=\mathrm{diag}(s)(W_0)_{:,J}(W_0)_{:,J}^\top\mathrm{diag}(s)$. HF PEFT OFT 0.14 to 0.15 (exact Cayley, input side) carried a trainable output scale `oft_s` and has the same form.
- *GOFT's official code* by default (`no_scaling=False`) adds a trainable input-side diagonal $g$, initialised at ones. Then $W=W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)G$ and $K_{\rm out}(W)=W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)^2W_0^\top$, whatever the Givens part $G$ is. This is the paper's GOFT* ablation, and it is not covered.
- *Output-side implementations* (LyCORIS diag-OFT at its defaults; HF PEFT OFT up to 0.13, which merged to $R^\top W_0$; HF OFT on embedding layers; ETHER with `flip_side=True`) have image in $O(m)\cdot W_0$. They preserve $K_{\rm in}$, the partial input Grams $W_{I,:}^\top W_{I,:}$ and the spectrum, not $K_{\rm out}$. ETHER's official code with `ether_nb>1` applies a different reflection to each output-row group, which keeps neuron norms and within-group angles but neither the cross-group cosines nor the spectrum.
- *HF PEFT's default OFT* since 0.18 (`use_cayley_neumann=True`, 5 terms) builds $R=I+2Q+2Q^2+2Q^3+Q^4=C(Q)(I-Q^4)$, where $C(Q)=(I+Q)(I-Q)^{-1}$ is the exact Cayley map (HF's exact branch evaluated at $-Q$). Hence $R^\top R=(I-Q^4)^2\ne I$, and the singular values of $R$ are $\lvert1-\theta_k^4\rvert$, where $\pm i\theta_k$ are the eigenvalues of $Q$: a systematic contraction for small $Q$, and $R$ is singular when some $\theta_k=1$. Already the adapted weight has $K_{\rm out}=W_0(I-Q^4)^2W_0^\top$, a drift of order $\lVert Q\rVert^4$, and merge cycles compound it. For $\lVert Q\rVert_{\rm op}\le2^{1/4}$ every factor is a contraction, so the drift is a monotone shrinkage of neuron norms and singular values (Thm V.3, corollary 4). In 0.16 and 0.17 the last coefficient was $2$, so $R^\top R=I+4Q^6+4Q^8$, still not orthogonal, with drift of order $\lVert Q\rVert^6$. (HF's docstring says the default is `False`; the dataclass default is `True`.)
- *Quasi-orthogonal variants* preserve neither $K_{\rm out}$ nor its normalised form exactly: qGOFT, whose $2\times2$ blocks are arbitrary matrices with only a soft orthogonality penalty; ETHER+, which is two-sided and whose factor $I-uu^\top+vv^\top$ is singular when $u\perp v$; and the default HF OFT above. Once $n>2$, a single non-orthogonal $2\times2$ block, conformal or not, generically changes some cosine.
- *Finite precision.* The invariance is exact only in exact arithmetic; low-precision merges add a rounding drift that grows like a random walk over merge cycles.

*Proof.* Use Thm II.5(1) and (3), and the fact that a product of orthogonal factors is orthogonal. $(WR)_{:,J}=W_{:,J}R_J$ for block-diagonal $R$. For BOFT, $K_{\rm out}(W)_{ij}=s_is_jK_{\rm out}(W_0)_{ij}$, so $\cos'_{ij}=\mathrm{sign}(s_is_j)\cos_{ij}$ when $s_is_j\ne0$. For GOFT with its scaler, $W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)GG^\top\mathrm{diag}(g)W_0^\top=W_0\mathrm{diag}(g)^2W_0^\top$. For the Cayley–Neumann map, $(I-Q)(I+Q+Q^2+Q^3)=I-Q^4$, $C(Q)$ is orthogonal and commutes with $Q^4$, and $Q^4$ is symmetric with eigenvalues $\theta_k^4$ for skew $Q$. ∎ [Num: with $s=(-0.5,1,\dots,1)$, BOFT changes a cosine by $1.5$ while $\lvert\cos\rvert$ is fixed to $4\times10^{-16}$, and an all-negative $s$ fixes every cosine to $10^{-15}$; GOFT with a scaler $g=1\pm0.05$ moves cosines and singular values; a conformal block $\sqrt2\,\mathrm{Rot}(0.3)$ on two of $8$ input coordinates changes a cosine; HF's default OFT satisfies $R^\top R=(I-Q^4)^2$ to $5\times10^{-16}$, with $\lVert R^\top R-I\rVert=1.5\times10^{-2}$ already for block size $8$ and skew entries of size about $0.07$, and is singular at $\theta=1$.]

*What this explains.* "Orthogonal fine-tuning preserves knowledge" corresponds to preserving a complete invariant: in exact arithmetic an exactly orthogonal method can *never* change spectra or neuron geometry, so a variant that must change them adds a non-orthogonal factor (the per-neuron scale of Qiu et al.'s re-scaled OFT, as in HF BOFT and HF OFT 0.14 to 0.15, or GOFT's default input scaler). This is a statement about reach, not a proof that preserving the invariant causes knowledge retention: ETHER's control study (Bini et al. 2024) found a non-orthogonal multiplicative variant comparably stable. Earlier claims that BOFT or qGOFT preserve the full Gram matrix are corrected here.

**Theorem II.7 (HRA is the meet of LoRA with the rotation orbit, and it starts at an apex).** [Thm] Let $W_0\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}$ be injective ($\operatorname{rank}W_0=n$), and let $r$ be even with $2\le r\le n-2$. Define $\mathrm{HRA}_r:(u_1,\dots,u_r)\mapsto W_0H_{u_1}\cdots H_{u_r}$ with $u_i\ne0$, where $H_u=I-2uu^\top/\|u\|^2$. Write $\mathrm{OFT}_{SO}:=W_0\cdot SO(n)$ for the rotation orbit. (The $O(n)$-orbit would add the odd component: $W_0H_{e_1}$ lies in $W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}$ but not in $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r$.)

(a) **Meet.** $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r=(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap W_0\cdot SO(n)$. Further, $d(\mathrm{HRA}_r)=rn-\tfrac{r(r+1)}2$, so the generic fibre has dimension $\tfrac{r(r+1)}2=rn-d$. This gauge is non-linear. The evident continuous global symmetries are the $r$ scalings $u_i\mapsto c_iu_i$; the braid group also acts globally by Hurwitz moves $(u_i,u_{i+1})\mapsto(H_{u_i}u_{i+1},u_i)$, since $H_aH_b=H_{H_ab}H_a$.

(b) **First-order collapse.** At the paired initialisation $u_{2i-1}=u_{2i}=w_i$ ($i\le k=r/2$, with the $w_i$ independent and $U=\mathrm{span}\{w_i\}$),
$$S_1=W_0\cdot\{\Omega\in\mathfrak{so}(n):P_{U^\perp}\Omega P_{U^\perp}=0\},\qquad\dim S_1=kn-\tfrac{k(k+1)}2<d(\mathrm{HRA}_r).$$
(c) **Vertex.** The germ of $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r$ at $W_0$ is diffeomorphic to the germ at $0$ of the skew-determinantal cone $\Sigma_r=\{\Omega\in\mathfrak{so}(n):\operatorname{rank}\Omega\le r\}$, which is not linear. So $W_0$ is a singular point (the vertex) of the image. Together with (b), the paired initialisation is an **apex**.

(d) **Scope.**
- *Not a product.* (a) is a meet in the poset of subsets of $\Theta$ containing $W_0$ (Obs I.4(c)). HRA is not the categorical product of LoRA$_r$ and OFT$_{SO}$: the set-level fibre product $\{(B,A,R):W_0+BA=W_0R\}$ has local dimension $d+r^2=rn+\tfrac{r(r-1)}2\ne rn$ over a generic point of the meet. (It is not pure-dimensional: over $W_0$ alone it contains $\{(B,0,I)\}$, of dimension $mr$.)
- *Exactly orthogonal OFT is smaller.* The Cayley map never produces a rotation with eigenvalue $-1$. For exact full-block Cayley OFT (the original paper; HF with `use_cayley_neumann=False`), $(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{OFT}$ is dense in $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r$ but not equal to it: $W_0H_{e_1}H_{e_2}$, with $H_{e_1}H_{e_2}=\mathrm{diag}(-1,-1,1,\dots,1)$, is missing. For exact block Cayley OFT with $b<n$ the intersection is a proper subset of $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r$.
- *HF's default OFT is incomparable.* HF PEFT's default OFT (`use_cayley_neumann=True`) is not orthogonal (Cor II.6). Since $R^\top R=(I-Q^4)^2=I$ forces $Q=0$ near $Q=0$, its image meets $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HRA}_r$ near $W_0$ only at $W_0$, while its intersection with $W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}$ contains non-orthogonal points such as $W_0(I+2Q+2Q^2+2Q^3+Q^4)$ with $\operatorname{rank}Q=2$.
- *Implementation.* The theorem concerns HRA with $\lambda<\infty$ (HF `apply_GS=False`). The strictly orthogonal Gram–Schmidt variant has the smaller image $W_0(I-2P_U)$, of dimension $r(n-r)$. The paired initialisation is HF's default (`repeat_interleave`) and is not stated in the HRA paper. HF HRA acts on `in_features`, so injectivity requires `out_features` $\ge$ `in_features` (and full column rank); the theorem does not cover `down_proj` or GQA key/value projections. The Gram–Schmidt variant never contains $W_0$, since $I-2P_U\ne I$, and at HF's default paired initialisation it runs Gram–Schmidt on duplicated columns, which is numerically degenerate.

*Proof.* (a) "$\subseteq$": each $H_u-I$ has rank one, so $R=\prod H_{u_i}$ fixes $\bigcap u_i^\perp$ and $\operatorname{rank}(R-I)\le r$. Also $\det R=(-1)^r=1$.

"$\supseteq$": suppose $W_0R=W_0+X$ with $\operatorname{rank}X\le r$ and $R\in SO(n)$. Injectivity gives $k':=\operatorname{rank}(R-I)\le r$. By the Cartan–Dieudonné theorem in Scherk's sharp form, $R$ is a product of exactly $k'$ reflections, and $\det R=(-1)^{k'}$ forces $k'$ to be even. Pad with $(r-k')/2$ pairs $H_uH_u=I$.

Dimension: a generic element is determined by its moved space $\mathrm{im}(R-I)\in\mathrm{Gr}(r,n)$, of dimension $r(n-r)$, together with a fixed-point-free element of $SO(r)$ on it, an open set of dimension $r(r-1)/2$. The generic fibre then has dimension $rn-d$.

(b) Normalise $\hat w=w/\|w\|$. A direct computation gives
$$\tfrac{d}{d\varepsilon}\big(H_{w+\varepsilon a}H_{w+\varepsilon b}\big)\big|_0=\tfrac{2}{\|w\|}\big(v\hat w^\top-\hat wv^\top\big),\qquad v=(I-\hat w\hat w^\top)(a-b).$$
Since every pair equals $I$ at $q_0$, the differential of the product is the sum of the pair differentials. So $S_1=W_0\cdot\{\sum_iv_i\wedge\hat w_i\}$, where $v\wedge w:=vw^\top-wv^\top$.

This set is contained in $\{\Omega:P_{U^\perp}\Omega P_{U^\perp}=0\}$, because $y^\top(v\hat w^\top-\hat wv^\top)x=0$ for $x,y\perp\hat w$. Conversely, the right-hand side is spanned by $e_a\wedge e_b$ with $e_a\in U$, and each of these is a combination of the elements $\hat w_i\wedge e_b$. Its dimension is $\dim\mathfrak{so}(n)-\dim\mathfrak{so}(n-k)$, and $W_0$ is injective. Finally,
$$d-\dim S_1=k(2n-3k-1)/2>0\quad\text{for }n\ge 2k+2.$$

(c) Use the Cayley map $C(\Omega)=(I+\Omega)(I-\Omega)^{-1}$. It is a diffeomorphism from a neighbourhood of $0\in\mathfrak{so}(n)$ onto a neighbourhood of $I\in SO(n)$, and $C(\Omega)-I=2\Omega(I-\Omega)^{-1}$, so $\operatorname{rank}(C(\Omega)-I)=\operatorname{rank}\Omega$. Composing with the linear embedding $R\mapsto W_0R$ identifies the germs. $\Sigma_r$ is a closed cone. It is not linear, because a rank-$r$ and a rank-$2$ skew matrix can sum to rank $r+2\le n$. A closed cone that is $C^1$ at its vertex is linear, so $0$ is singular. The apex statement is (b): a singular image alone would not give a positive deficit.

(d) Over a generic point of the meet, the LoRA fibre is a $GL_r$-orbit and the $SO(n)$ fibre is a point, since $W_0$ is injective. If $C(\Omega)x=-x$, then $y=(I-\Omega)^{-1}x$ satisfies $(I+\Omega)y=-(I-\Omega)y$, so $y=0$. Density: perturb the angles $\pi$ inside the same moved planes. The Gram–Schmidt variant uses orthonormal $\hat u_i$, and $\prod(I-2\hat u_i\hat u_i^\top)=I-2P_U$. ∎ [Num: at $n=9$, the paired-init Jacobian has rank $8$ against generic rank $15$ for $r=2$, and $15$ against $26$ for $r=4$.]

*What this explains.*
- HRA's "bridge between low-rank and orthogonal adaptation" is, at the level of images, the meet of LoRA with the rotation orbit $W_0\cdot SO(n)$. It is not a product of methods. It is strictly larger than LoRA $\cap$ OFT for exactly orthogonal (Cayley) OFT, as in the original paper or HF with `use_cayley_neumann=False`; for HF's default Cayley–Neumann OFT the two are incomparable.
- HF recommends an even $r$ because its paired initialisation needs pairs, and for odd $r$ it falls back to a random Kaiming init. The determinant identity explains why no odd-$r$ HRA can start at $W_0$, so that fallback is a pointing defect.
- New here (earlier analyses labelled this base point smooth): **HRA's default initialisation is an apex**. At first order, each pair can only rotate its own vector against the rest of the space. This is the multiplicative twin of LoRA's dead $A$-direction, so HRA belongs in the apex column beside LoRA.

**Proposition II.8 (DoRA is LoRA followed by an output scale, at the level of images).** [Prop] Assume $1\le r\le\min(m,n)$ and $s\ne0$. Let $N$ normalise rows; DoRA's magnitude $\mu\in\mathbb R^m$ is per output neuron, as in HF PEFT and the official code (the paper's notation says "column-wise", in the transposed convention). Images refer to the merged (eval-mode) weight; HF's train-mode forward with `lora_dropout`$>0$ is not of the form $Wx$. Let $W_0$ have no zero row, let $\mathcal U$ be the open set of matrices with no zero row, and let $\mathrm{Diag}_m$ be the monoid of all diagonal matrices. Then
$$\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{DoRA}_r=\{\mathrm{diag}(\mu)\,N(V):V\in(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap\mathcal U,\ \mu\in\mathbb R^m\}=\mathrm{Diag}_m\cdot(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}),$$
which is exactly the image of "LoRA$_r$ followed by a trainable output scale $\ell\in\mathbb R^m$". With $\mu$ restricted to $(\mathbb R^\times)^m$, or equivalently after intersecting with $\mathcal U$: $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{DoRA}_r\cap\mathcal U=T_m\cdot(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})\cap\mathcal U$. Moreover:
- $d(\mathrm{DoRA}_r)\le\min\big(mn,\ r(m+n-r)+m\big)$. At a parameter point with $\mu\in(\mathbb R^\times)^m$ and $X=sBA\in\mathcal M_r$, with $U=\mathrm{col}X$ and $R=\mathrm{row}X$, the Jacobian rank is $r(m+n-r)+m-\dim\ker\big(\delta\mapsto P_{U^\perp}\mathrm{diag}(\delta)W_0P_{R^\perp}\big)$; where some $\mu_i=0$ it is smaller when $r\ge2$, and for $r=1$ when some row $w_{0,i}$ with $\mu_i=0$ lies outside $R$. For generic $W_0$, $d=\min\big(mn,\ r(m+n-r)+m\big)$; in particular $d=r(m+n-r)+m$ iff $(m-r)(n-r)\ge m$, which holds for all practical shapes. For special $W_0$ it is smaller: for $W_0=ac^\top$ with all $a_i\ne0$ and $r<n$, $d=r(m+n-r)+m-r$, strictly below the generic value iff $r<m$ and $r\le n-2$.
- The image covariance group contains $\mathrm{Mon}_m\times GL_n$.
- At $B=0$, in HF's magnitude coordinates, $\Delta W=(\mathrm{diag}(\mu_i/\|w_{0,i}\|)-I)W_0$, which can have rank up to $\operatorname{rank}W_0$, so DoRA escapes LoRA's rank bound.

*Proof.* $\mathrm{diag}(\mu)N(V)=\mathrm{diag}(\mu_i/\|v_i\|)V\in\mathrm{Diag}_m\cdot V$. Conversely, take $\mathrm{diag}(\ell)V$ with $V=W_0+X$, $\operatorname{rank}X\le r$, and let $Z$ be the set of zero rows of $V$. Put $V'=W_0+(I-\varepsilon\sum_{i\in Z}E_{ii})X$. Its rows outside $Z$ are those of $V$, its rows in $Z$ are $\varepsilon w_{0,i}\ne0$, and its update still has rank $\le r$. With $\ell'_i=0$ on $Z$ and $\ell'_i=\ell_i$ elsewhere, $\mathrm{diag}(\ell')V'=\mathrm{diag}(\ell)V$ and $V'\in\mathcal U$. For the $T_m$ statement, $DV\in\mathcal U$ with $D$ invertible forces $V\in\mathcal U$. Dimension: $\varphi(\mu,B,A)=(\mu_i/\lVert v_i\rVert,B,A)$ is a diffeomorphism of DoRA's domain onto that of LoRA plus an output scale, and $\rho_{\rm DoRA}=\rho_{\text{LoRA+scale}}\circ\varphi$. The tangent space at $(D,X)$ of $(D,X)\mapsto D(W_0+X)$ is $D\big[\{\mathrm{diag}(\delta)V\}+T_X\mathcal M_r\big]$ after $\delta\mapsto D^{-1}\delta$, so for invertible $D$ it suffices to work at $(I,X)$. There $\{\mathrm{diag}(\delta)V\}$ has dimension $m$ because $V\in\mathcal U$, and $\mathrm{diag}(\delta)V\in T_X\mathcal M_r$ iff $P_{U^\perp}\mathrm{diag}(\delta)VP_{R^\perp}=0$, where $VP_{R^\perp}=W_0P_{R^\perp}$. Zero magnitudes: $\mu_i=0$ iff $\ell_i=0$ under $\varphi$; let $Z_0=\{i:\ell_i=0\}\ne\emptyset$, write $\mathcal S=\{\mathrm{diag}(\delta)V\}$ and $T=T_X\mathcal M_r$, and let $F=\dim(\mathcal S+T)$ be the value of the formula. In the image of the differential $(\delta,\dot B,\dot A)\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(\delta)V+\mathrm{diag}(\ell)\,s(\dot BA+B\dot A)$ the rows in $Z_0$ are $\delta_iv_i$, so its dimension is $\lvert Z_0\rvert$ plus that of the projection of $\mathcal S+T$ to the other rows. The part of $\mathcal S+T$ supported on the rows in $Z_0$ contains every $\sum_{i\in Z_0}e_i(c_iv_i+y_i)^\top$ with $y_i\in R$, and $v_i-w_{0,i}\in R$, so the rank is at most $F-\sum_{i\in Z_0}\big(\dim\mathrm{span}(w_{0,i},R)-1\big)\le F-\lvert Z_0\rvert(r-1)$. This is below $F$ when $r\ge2$, or when some $w_{0,i}$ with $i\in Z_0$ lies outside $R$; for $r=1$ with every such row in $R$ it can equal $F$. Generic value: with bases $E$ of $U^\perp$ and $F$ of $R^\perp$ the map is $\delta\mapsto\sum_i\delta_i\,(E^\top e_i)\otimes(F^\top w_{0,i})$. For generic $(W_0,U,R)$ these are $m$ generic points of the Segre cone in $\mathbb R^{m-r}\otimes\mathbb R^{n-r}$, which is irreducible and spans the whole space, so they span $\min(m,(m-r)(n-r))$ dimensions (a standard-basis witness shows the open condition is non-empty). Since $r(m+n-r)+(m-r)(n-r)=mn$, $d=\min(mn,r(m+n-r)+m)$ off a proper algebraic subset. For $W_0=ac^\top$, the kernel is $\mathrm{diag}(a)^{-1}U$, of dimension $r$, whenever $c\notin R$. Covariance: a monomial $g$ normalises $\mathrm{Diag}_m$ and $g\mathcal M_{\le r}h^{-1}=\mathcal M_{\le r}$, so $g\,\mathrm{Diag}_m(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})h^{-1}=\mathrm{Diag}_m(gW_0h^{-1}+\mathcal M_{\le r})$. ∎ [Num: Jacobian rank $9=mn$ at $(m,n,r)=(3,3,2)$, where the formula gives $11$; $36$ at $(8,8,2)$, matching, and $34$ there when one $\mu_i=0$; $24$ for a rank-one $W_0$ at $(6,6,2)$.]

*What this explains.* At the level of images, DoRA's "magnitude–direction decoupling" is LoRA plus $m$ free output gains. What DoRA changes beyond that lives in its training dynamics. Under gradient flow or SGD there are two different geometries. The exact gradient of the normalised parametrisation projects the direction gradient row by row, which is a change of metric. The detached backward of the DoRA paper (Sec. 4.3) and of HF PEFT (`weight_norm.detach()`) removes that projection; the resulting update is a non-gradient pseudo-gradient, not a metric, and need not be a descent direction. Both the paper and HF train with AdamW, under which neither variant follows a fixed metric, and the coordinates matter in their own right: an equal step on $\mu_i$ (of size about $\lVert w_{0,i}\rVert$) and on a gain $\ell_i$ (of size $1$) changes the row gain by different amounts. **[Experiment D]** With one optimizer (AdamW, same learning rate, weight decay and data order) and the same $W_0$, compare four arms: (i) LoRA + gain $\ell$ with $\ell_0=\mathbf 1$; (ii) LoRA + gain in DoRA's coordinates, $W=\mathrm{diag}(\mu_i/\lVert w_{0,i}\rVert)(W_0+sBA)$ with $\mu_0=(\lVert w_{0,i}\rVert)_i$, which isolates the optimizer-coordinate effect without normalisation; (iii) DoRA with the exact backward; (iv) DoRA with HF's detached backward. Since the images are equal, any gap between arms comes from the parametrisation, the backward rule or the optimizer coordinates, and the four arms separate these. The DoRA paper's own ablation reports (iii) and (iv) within $0.2$ points.

**Proposition II.9 (the torus ladder).** [Prop]

(a) **HiRA.** $\rho(B,A)=W_0\odot(J+BA)$, where $J$ is the all-ones matrix; HF PEFT has $B_0=0$ and no scale factor on Linear and Conv layers (on embeddings it uses $A_0=0$). For every $W_0$, $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HiRA}_r=W_0+D_{W_0}(\mathcal M_{\le r})$ with $D_{W_0}:X\mapsto W_0\odot X$. If $W_0$ has no zero entries, $D_{W_0}\in GL(\Theta)$, so inside the slice at $\theta_0$ HiRA is LoRA transported by a diagonal automorphism: it has the same $d=r(m+n-r)$ (for $r\le\min(m,n)$) and the same gauge. In all cases $\operatorname{rank}\Delta W\le r\cdot\operatorname{rank}W_0$. The transport depends on $\theta_0$ (Prop II.13).

(b) **Scalings.** Output-side scalings, $\mathrm{diag}(\ell)W_0=W_0\odot(J+(\ell-\mathbf 1)\mathbf 1^\top)$, and input-side scalings, $W_0\mathrm{diag}(\ell)=W_0\odot(J+\mathbf 1(\ell-\mathbf 1)^\top)$, have images in $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HiRA}_1$. Output-side examples: (IA)$^3$ on keys and values, the post-linear scale of SSF, DoRA's magnitude. Input-side examples: (IA)$^3$'s feed-forward vector, which scales the input of $W_{\rm down}$, and a LayerNorm gain $\gamma$ folded into the next linear map. The fold is exact when the norm's output is consumed only by linear maps: the input norms of pre-norm blocks, and a final norm before an untied head (in post-norm BERT, the last block's norm feeds only the head). It does not apply to Gemma-style post-sublayer norms, which HF's default LN-tuning targets for Gemma 2 and 3, to QK-norms, or to post-norm residual paths. Where it applies, $\gamma$ is tied across all consumers of the norm (Q, K and V, or gate and up) rather than being a free point per box, and with tied embeddings a fold into the head breaks the tie. LayerNorm biases and SSF's shift fold into the next bias, not into $W$. Two-sided scalings $\mathrm{diag}(a)W_0\mathrm{diag}(b)=W_0\odot(ab^\top)$ lie in $\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{HiRA}_2$. As a morphism, $h(\ell)=(\ell-\mathbf 1,\mathbf 1^\top)$ lands in $\mathrm{HiRA}_1$ pointed at $(0,\mathbf 1^\top)$, which is not HiRA's random-$A$ default pointing.

(c) **Orbit dimension.** The two-sided torus orbit $\{\mathrm{diag}(a)W_0\mathrm{diag}(b)\}$ has dimension $m+n-c(W_0)$, where $c(W_0)$ is the number of connected components of the bipartite support graph of $W_0$, an isolated vertex counting as its own component.

(d) **RoAd.** RoAd$_1$ (the paper's main variant, HF `road_1`) is $W=RW_0$ with $R$ block-diagonal in scaled rotations $\alpha_i\mathrm{Rot}(\theta_i)$ of output pairs. It is the left action of $CO^+(2)^{m/2}\cong(\mathbb C^\times)^{m/2}$: it is **(IA)$^3$ over $\mathbb C$**. It multiplies the input Gram matrix of each output pair by $\alpha_i^2$. Since $\alpha_i$ may be $0$, its image is the orbit closure, a real-linear space of dimension exactly $m$ whenever no output-pair block of $W_0$ is zero. RoAd$_2$ and RoAd$_4$ (HF `road_2`, `road_4`) let each block range over all of $M_2(\mathbb R)$. They realise the block-diagonal action of the monoid $M_2(\mathbb R)^{m/2}$ (its group part $GL_2^{m/2}$ is non-abelian), with linear image $\{\mathrm{blockdiag}(R_i)W_0\}$ of dimension $2m$ when every pair block has rank $2$. For them the Gram statement and "over $\mathbb C$" fail, and RoAd$_4$ carries a positive-dimensional gauge (two dimensions per row). HF pairs output $j$ with $j+g/2$ inside each group of size $g$ (default $64$) rather than adjacent outputs as in the paper, and also rotates the bias; the statements above do not depend on the pairing.

(e) **Zeros and covariance.** Every $W$ reachable by HiRA, (IA)$^3$ or SSF vanishes wherever $W_0$ does, $Z(W_0)\subseteq Z(W)$, and this persists under merging. New zeros can appear ($\ell_i=0$, or $(J+BA)_{ij}=0$), so the zero pattern itself is not invariant. The image covariance group of output-scaled boxes ((IA)$^3$ on $k,v$; post-linear SSF) is $\mathrm{Mon}_m\times GL_n$, and that of input-scaled boxes ((IA)$^3$ on $W_{\rm down}$) is $GL_m\times\mathrm{Mon}_n$. HiRA's is exactly $\mathrm{Mon}_m\times\mathrm{Mon}_n$ ($=GL_1\times GL_1$ when $m=n=1$).

*Proof.* (a) $W_0\odot(J+BA)=W_0+W_0\odot BA$. $D_{W_0}$ is diagonal in the standard basis of $\Theta$, and invertible iff no entry vanishes. Pushforward along a linear isomorphism preserves dimension and gauge. For the rank, $W_0\odot(BA)=\sum_k\mathrm{diag}(b_k)W_0\mathrm{diag}(a_k)$, which has rank $\le r\operatorname{rank}W_0$.

(b) Direct computation, using $ab^\top-J=[a,-\mathbf 1][b,\mathbf 1]^\top\in\mathcal M_{\le2}$.

(c) The tangent map at $(\mathbf 1,\mathbf 1)$ is $(\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\big((\alpha_i+\beta_j)(W_0)_{ij}\big)$. Its kernel requires $\alpha_i+\beta_j=0$ along every support edge, so it is one-dimensional on each connected component. Torus orbits are homogeneous, so the dimension is the same at every point of the orbit.

(d) A $2\times n$ real block $X$ is a complex row in $\mathbb C^n$, and $\alpha\mathrm{Rot}(\theta)$ acts on it as multiplication by $\alpha e^{i\theta}$. So the image for one pair is $\mathbb C\cdot X$, of real dimension $2$ iff $X\ne0$, and $(\alpha\mathrm{Rot}X)^\top(\alpha\mathrm{Rot}X)=\alpha^2X^\top X$. In RoAd$_2$ each row of a block is an arbitrary vector of $\mathbb R^2$ written in polar form.

(e) Hadamard multiplication and row or column scaling preserve the zeros of $W_0$.

*(IA)$^3$ and SSF.* A monomial $g$ normalises the diagonal monoid, so $\mathrm{Mon}_m\times GL_n$ is contained in the covariance group of output scalings. Conversely, take $W_0=e_jc^\top$. Then $\mathrm{Im}\,M(gW_0h^{-1})=\mathrm{span}\{e_i:i\in\mathrm{supp}(ge_j)\}\,c^\top h^{-1}$, while $g\,\mathrm{Im}\,M(W_0)\,h^{-1}=\mathbb R\,(ge_j)c^\top h^{-1}$. Equality forces $\lvert\mathrm{supp}(ge_j)\rvert=1$ for every $j$, so $g$ is monomial. This works for all $m,n$. Input-side scalings are the transpose.

*HiRA.* Monomial pairs commute with Hadamard products up to relabelling. Conversely, take $W_0=E_{ij}$, so that $\mathrm{Im}\,M(E_{ij})=\mathbb RE_{ij}$, and put $u=ge_i$, $v=h^{-\top}e_j$. Covariance would force $\mathrm{Im}\,M(uv^\top)=uv^\top\odot(J+\mathcal M_{\le r})$ to be the line $\mathbb R\,uv^\top$. It contains $uv^\top+t\,u_kv_lE_{kl}$ for every $(k,l)$ in the support of $uv^\top$, so it has dimension at least $2$ unless $\lvert\mathrm{supp}\,u\rvert=\lvert\mathrm{supp}\,v\rvert=1$. This holds for every $i,j$ only when $g$ and $h$ are monomial. ∎ [Num: RoAd$_1$ orbit dimension $8$ and RoAd$_2$ dimension $16$ at $m=8$.]

*What this explains.* Each scaling method here is a rung $W_0\odot(J+Z)$: (IA)$^3$, SSF's scales and the gain part of LN-tuning on norms that feed only linear maps lie in $\mathrm{HiRA}_1$, on one side or the other, and HiRA is LoRA in the Hadamard frame of $W_0$. DoRA's image is the $\mathrm{Diag}_m$-orbit of LoRA's image (image level only; Prop II.8). DoRA and RoAd can fill zeros of $W_0$, so they become rungs only when $W_0$ is zero-free. Then every image germ can be written as $W_0\odot(J+Z)$, so the "ladder" is bookkeeping rather than a constraint. RoAd$_1$ is complex (IA)$^3$ and RoAd$_{2,4}$ are block-$M_2(\mathbb R)$ (IA)$^3$; for generic $W_0$ neither lies on a low-rank rung.

### II.C Cut and volume

**Theorem II.10 (cut-rank bound).** [Thm; standard] Let $\Gamma$ be a tensor network, evaluated in the symmetric monoidal category $(\mathbf{FinVect},\otimes)$, with input legs $V$ and output legs $U$. Every wire and open leg is assumed incident to a vertex: insert an identity box on any bare strand, or equivalently count each bare input–output wire as crossing every cut. A crossing hyperedge (copy spider) contributes its dimension once. Partition the vertices into $\mathcal I\sqcup\mathcal O$. The weight $w$ of the cut is the product of the dimensions of the internal wires between the two sides and of the open legs attached on the wrong side (input legs at $\mathcal O$, output legs at $\mathcal I$). Then
$$\operatorname{rank}[\![\Gamma]\!]\le\min_{\rm cuts}w,$$
