Rows of $A$ are symmetric.

(c) Prop III.4(a); heavy-ball momentum transforms like the step. Damping fails for non-orthogonal $g$ because $g^{-1}(gAA^\top g^\top+\delta I)g^{-\top}=AA^\top+\delta g^{-1}g^{-\top}$. For LoRA-RITE we follow Algorithm 1 of Yen et al. line by line on the full-rank locus, where its pseudo-inverses are inverses. In their notation the update is $AB^\top$ with $A\in\mathbb R^{m\times r}$ and $B\in\mathbb R^{n\times r}$, and the gauge is $A_2=A_1R$, $B_2=B_1R^{-\top}$ with $R\in GL_r$. The rule is symmetric in $A$ and $B$. For the step of $A$ it writes $B=U_BR_B$ by polar decomposition ($U_B$ with orthonormal columns) and forms, at step $t$, the unmagnified gradient $\bar\nabla_{A,t}=\nabla_{A,t}R_{B_t}^{-1}$, the basis change $P_{A,t}=U_{B_t}^\top U_{B_{t-1}}$, a second moment $\bar V_{A,t}$ that combines $P_{A,t}\bar V_{A,t-1}P_{A,t}^\top$ and $\bar\nabla_{A,t}^\top\bar\nabla_{A,t}$ with fixed weights, the escaped mass $\rho_{A,t}=\rho_{A,t-1}+\max_i\lvert\lambda_i(\bar V_{A,t-1})-\lambda_i(P_{A,t}\bar V_{A,t-1}P_{A,t}^\top)\rvert$, which depends only on spectra, the preconditioned step $\bar S_{A,t}=\bar\nabla_{A,t}(\bar V_{A,t}+\rho_{A,t}I)^{-1/2}$, the first moment $\bar M_{A,t}=\beta_1\bar M_{A,t-1}P_{A,t}^\top+(1-\beta_1)\bar S_{A,t}$ and the step $\delta A_t=-\eta\,\bar M_{A,t}R_{B_t}^{-\top}$. Run it from $(A_1,B_1)$ and from $(A_1R,B_1R^{-\top})$ with zero initial moments and escaped mass, and suppose that at step $t$ the parameters are related by $R$ and the states by $\bar V^{(2)}_{t-1}=O_{t-1}^\top\bar V^{(1)}_{t-1}O_{t-1}$, $\bar M^{(2)}_{t-1}=\bar M^{(1)}_{t-1}O_{t-1}$ and $\rho^{(2)}_{t-1}=\rho^{(1)}_{t-1}$ (true at $t=1$). Equal column spaces give $U_{B_{2,t}}=U_{B_{1,t}}O_t$ with $O_t\in O(r)$ and $R_{B_{2,t}}=O_t^\top R_{B_{1,t}}R^{-\top}$, and $\nabla_{A_2}=\nabla_{A_1}R^{-\top}$, so $\bar\nabla_{A_2,t}=\bar\nabla_{A_1,t}O_t$ and $P^{(2)}_{A,t}=O_t^\top P^{(1)}_{A,t}O_{t-1}$. Hence $\bar V^{(2)}_t=O_t^\top\bar V^{(1)}_tO_t$, the escaped masses agree because conjugation keeps eigenvalues, $\bar S^{(2)}_t=\bar S^{(1)}_tO_t$, $\bar M^{(2)}_t=\bar M^{(1)}_tO_t$, and $\delta A_{2,t}=-\eta\,\bar M^{(1)}_tO_tO_t^\top R_{B_{1,t}}^{-\top}R=\delta A_{1,t}R$, the transported step; the step of $B$ is the same computation with $R^{-\top}$ in place of $R$. This closes the induction. The resulting step agrees with Eq. 15 of Yen et al., $\delta A_2=\delta A_1B_1^\top(B_2^\top)^+$, since $(B_2^\top)^+=B_1(B_1^\top B_1)^{-1}R$ when $B_1$ has full column rank. ∎ [Num: defects $10^{-15}$ for signed permutations, $11.3$ for a positive diagonal, $6.6$ for a rotation, and $5\times10^{-16}$ for the diagonal with jointly rescaled per-channel rates at $\varepsilon=0$; LoRA-RITE (Alg. 1) from $(B,A)$ and $(Bg^{-1},gA)$ with a generic $g$ agrees to $3\times10^{-14}$ over 30 steps (referee replication); with $\varepsilon=10^{-8}$ left unrescaled the joint torus fails by about $2\%$; LoRA-Pro with the Sylvester $X$ has a parameter-level defect of $1.2$ under a non-orthogonal $g$, against $2\times10^{-15}$ with $X=0$ or with an orthogonal $g$.]

*What this explains.* There is a hierarchy $B_r\subset O(r)\subset GL_r$ of optimizer geometries, strict for $r\ge2$. $GL_r$-equivariant rules, started from gauge-related states, define an optimizer on the *model* rather than on a chart. More generally any rule that maps gauge orbits to gauge orbits does (for instance a step followed by a canonical refactorisation of $W$), while $B_r$- and $O(r)$-equivariant rules in general do not. Plain Adam, the default, sees a finite shadow of the gauge. LoRA+ and rsLoRA act on the hyperparameter torus (Thm III.10), not on the gauge of a fixed method; balanced or orthonormal initialisations choose a point in the fibre. The positive torus is a symmetry of the *hyperparameters*, not of Adam at fixed rates; this corrects an earlier claim.

**Corollary III.13 (what a zero-$B$ initialisation really chooses).** [Cor] At $q_0=(0,A_0)$ with $\operatorname{rank}A_0=r$, with hyperparameters shared by the rank channels and the same data order and dropout masks, two choices of $A_0$ give the same $\theta$-trajectory whenever they are related by the optimizer's gauge group. The resulting invariant of $A_0$ is:
- $\mathrm{row}A_0\in\mathrm{Gr}(r,n)$ for an update rule that is $GL_r$-equivariant at every point of its trajectory, including $B=0$ and rank-deficient $B$, run without global-norm clipping. An example is $\delta B=-\eta\nabla_B(AA^\top)^{-1}$, $\delta A=-\eta(AA^\top)\nabla_A$, which needs only $\operatorname{rank}A=r$;
- the PSD matrix $A_0^\top A_0$ for SGD, which is exactly the initial preconditioner $\dot W(0)=-s^2\eta_BGA_0^\top A_0$;
- $A_0$ up to signed permutation of its rows for Adam.

The undamped scaled-GD optimizers of Thm III.12(c) are not defined at $B=0$, since they need $(B^\top B)^{-1}$. LoRA-RITE uses pseudo-inverses and so is defined there, but its $GL_r$-equivariance is established only on the full-rank locus. Their damped forms are only $O(r)$-equivariant, so in practice their invariant is a $\delta$-dependent function of $A_0^\top A_0$, and their Adam-wrapped forms (Riemannian-preconditioned AdamW, HF's LoRA-FA optimizer) have the invariant $A_0$ modulo $B_r$. Conversely, different invariants give different dynamics, for SGD and for Adam at $\varepsilon=0$.

Random-$A$ LoRA and EVA are different points of the $B=0$ stratum $\{(0,A):\operatorname{rank}A=r\}$ of the apex fibre $\rho^{-1}(\theta_0)=\{(B,A):BA=0\}$; Init[B] lies in another stratum of the same fibre. LoRA-FA is a different *object*: the sub-object $\mathrm{FA}(A_0)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{LoRA}_{(0,A_0)}$. More precisely, EVA is a point of the apex fibre of LoRA$_{r_l}$ at its per-layer redistributed rank and adjusted $\alpha$, with orthonormal rows only when `whiten=False`.

*Proof.* Only the pointwise "$\supseteq$" half of Thm III.12 is used. For SGD and Adam it holds off the full-rank locus, and the initial Adam state $(m,v)=0$ is fixed by $B_r$. With $B_0=0$ the term $B_0g^{-1}$ stays $0$, so equivariant updates give identical $W$-trajectories. Left $O(r)$-orbits of full-rank $A_0$ are classified by $A_0^\top A_0$ (Thm II.5(2)), and $GL_r$-orbits by the row space (proof of Thm II.2). For the example rule, $\nabla_{A'}=g^{-\top}\nabla_A$ and $A'A'^\top=gAA^\top g^\top$ give $\delta A'=g\,\delta A$, and $\delta B'=\delta Bg^{-1}$ as in Prop III.4(a). Orthogonal $g$ preserve the gradient norms, so global-norm clipping factors agree in the two runs for SGD and Adam. *Converse, by comparing first steps.* For SGD the first step from $(0,A_0)$ is $\delta W_1=-s^2\eta_BGA_0^\top A_0$, so equal trajectories for every linear loss force $A_0^\top A_0=A_0'^\top A_0'$. For Adam at $\varepsilon=0$ it is $\delta W_1=-s\eta_B\,\mathrm{sign}(GA_0^\top)A_0$ (a positive multiple of this without bias correction), so row by row $F(x)=\sum_j\mathrm{sign}(x\cdot a_j)\,a_j$ must equal $F'(x)=\sum_j\mathrm{sign}(x\cdot a_j')\,a_j'$ for every row vector $x$, where $a_j$ and $a_j'$ are the rows of $A_0$ and $A_0'$. The rows are independent, so the hyperplanes $a_j^\perp$ are distinct and each contains points that lie on no other; $F$ is constant off $\bigcup_ja_j^\perp$, and crossing $a_j^\perp$ at such a point changes it by $2a_j\ne0$. So $\bigcup_ja_j^\perp=\bigcup_ka_k'^\perp$, there is a permutation $\pi$ with $a'_{\pi(j)}=c_ja_j$, comparing the jumps gives $\lvert c_j\rvert=1$, and $A_0'$ is a signed row permutation of $A_0$. For the example $GL_r$-rule the first step $-\eta s^2GP_{\mathrm{row}A_0}$ determines $\mathrm{row}A_0$. ∎ [Num: SGD from $A_0$ and $QA_0$ agree to $10^{-15}$, from $A_0$ and $DA_0$ differ by $0.81$; Adam from $A_0$ and $PA_0$ agree to $10^{-16}$, from $A_0$ and $QA_0$ differ by $0.98$; the example rule from $A_0$ and $gA_0$ agrees to $3\times10^{-15}$.]

*What this explains.* The strata of Thm II.2 are the expressive quotient. The dynamics see a finer quotient, and how fine depends on the optimizer.

---

## Exposé IV. The lens: tangent and cotangent methods

Backpropagation is the 2-functor $\mathbf{Para}(\mathsf R):\mathbf{Para}(\mathcal C)\to\mathbf{Para}(\mathbf{Lens}(\mathcal C))$, $f\mapsto(f,\mathsf R[f])$, with $\mathsf R[f](x,y)=J_f(x)^\top y$ (Cruttwell et al.; Fong–Spivak–Tuyéras). A reparametrisation therefore acts on both sides of the parameter port. On the tangent side its image is what the method can *express*. On the cotangent side, the pulled-back gradient $\mathsf R[\rho]$ induces the cometric $K$, which is how the method *moves*.

**Definition IV.1 (forward and backward methods).** [Def]
- A **forward** method acts on the parameter port by a lens in the image of $\mathsf R$, namely $(\rho,\mathsf R[\rho])$.
- A **backward** (cotangent-side) method acts by a lens whose forward part is $\mathrm{id}_\Theta$ and whose backward part is a *compression* $c_t:T^*_\theta\Theta\to S$, paired with an *anchor* $a_t:S\to T_\theta\Theta$. The update is $\theta\leftarrow\theta-\eta\,a_t(\mathrm{opt}(c_t(\nabla L)))$.

Examples:

| method | compression $c_t$ | anchor $a_t$ |
|---|---|---|
| GaLore | $P_t^\top$, with $P_t$ from a gradient SVD | $P_t$ |
| GaLore with a random projector (Torroba-Hennigen et al.; Hao–Cao–Mou) | as GaLore, with $P_t$ random | $P_t$ |
| Flora | identity: only the momentum or accumulation buffer is compressed, by a random $A_t$, inside `opt` | identity (full-space Adafactor step) |
| LISA | layer-block coordinate projection | the same projection |
| MeZO | $g\mapsto\langle z_t,g\rangle$, estimated by finite differences without a backward pass | $\lambda\mapsto\lambda z_t$ |

**Proposition IV.2 (the degree-one collapse).** [Prop; Known: Torroba-Hennigen–Lang–Guo–Kim 2025 for GaLore (Thm 1, Cor. 3; App. B for weight decay); Hao–Cao–Mou 2024 for random projections] Let $\rho_k(C)=W_k+\mathcal LC$ with $\mathcal L$ linear and $C_0=0$. Let `opt` be a first-order optimizer, with no coupled or decoupled weight decay, whose state and output depend only on the sequence of gradients it is fed, and which does not read the parameter tensor: SGD, heavy-ball or Nesterov momentum, Adam/AMSGrad, or Adafactor with `scale_parameter=False` (fixed or relative step, with its factorisation applied to the compressed gradient in the same tensor shape as $C$). Run both procedures below with the same implementation of `opt`, on the same minibatches, and from the same optimizer state, fresh or carried over. Then they produce the same $W$-trajectory over a period:
- training $C$ with `opt` and merging at the end of the period;
- the backward method with $c=\mathcal L^*$ and $a=\mathcal L$, started at $W_k$.

AdamW with $\lambda>0$, LAMB/LARS trust ratios, parameter-scaled Adafactor (HF's default `scale_parameter=True`) and global-norm clipping of the full gradient (such as HF Trainer's default `max_grad_norm=1.0`, whenever it triggers) all break the identity. Injectivity of $\mathcal L$ is not needed.

In particular, on each matrix that GaLore projects (its other parameters train in full in both procedures), GaLore with a projector $P_k$ fixed on each period equals one-sided LoRA $W_k+P_kC$ (frozen $B=P_k$, or frozen $A$ when projecting on the right), merged and re-based every period, with $C$'s optimizer state (moments and step counter) carried over unchanged across re-basings rather than reset. $P_k$ must be exactly GaLore's matrix, signs and column basis included, not just its span, because the state is reused without transport. ReLoRA-style resets give a different trajectory. The identity is exact with weight decay $0$, no full-gradient clipping, and an optimizer from the admissible class; galore_torch's `GaLoreAdafactor` defaults to `scale_parameter=True` and is excluded, and `GaLoreAdamW` is the transformers-style AdamW (it adds $\varepsilon$ to $\sqrt v$ before bias correction), so the LoRA side must use the same implementation. GaLore's scale $\alpha$ multiplies the optimizer output, so it is a learning-rate factor, not a LoRA-style scale inside $\rho$; the two differ under Adam with $\varepsilon>0$.

*Proof.* By induction, $W_t=W_k+\mathcal LC_t$. Both procedures feed `opt` the same sequence $\mathcal L^*\nabla L(W_t)$ from the same state, so they receive the same outputs $U_t$. One sets $W\leftarrow W-\eta\mathcal LU_t$, the other $C\leftarrow C-\eta U_t$. The excluded ingredients see different tensors in the two procedures: decoupled decay acts on $C$ (pulling $W$ toward $W_k$) in one and on $W$ (toward $0$) in the other; parameter-scaled steps read $\mathrm{RMS}(C)$ in one and $\mathrm{RMS}(W)$ in the other; full-gradient clipping uses $\|G\|$ in one and $\|\mathcal L^*G\|$ in the other. ∎ [Num: $7\times10^{-16}$ under Adam over one period; $5\times10^{-15}$ over four periods with state carried on both sides, against $0.37$ when the LoRA side resets; $0.38$ for HF-default Adafactor; with one column of $P_k$ sign-flipped and state carried, the trajectories separate.]

*What this explains.* Beyond one-sided LoRA, GaLore adds two ingredients: the gradient-SVD frame, and the carry-over of optimizer state across re-basings. The carry-over is not a transport: moments expressed in the coordinates of $P_{k-1}$ are reused as if they were in those of $P_k$. Projection-aware state updates (e.g. LDAdam) add an actual transport. The equivalence classifies the cotangent methods whose optimizer satisfies its hypotheses; the LoRA and ReLoRA rows are contrast rows outside it:

| projector \ schedule | frozen | re-based periodically | resampled every step |
|---|---|---|---|
| random | LoRA-FA ($K=GA_0^\top A_0$ under SGD; HF's LoRA-FA optimizer gives $GP_V$) | GaLore with a random projector | MeZO (rank 1, global; up to finite-difference error) |
| gradient / data SVD | EVA, LoRA-GA (first order only) | GaLore | GaLore with $T=1$ |
| coordinate blocks | BitFit, FISH, LN-tuning | LISA | block coordinate descent |
| two-sided, state-dependent | LoRA | ReLoRA | — |

Flora proper is not in the table. It compresses only the momentum or the gradient-accumulation buffer by a random projection, runs Adafactor on the full gradient (with parameter scaling by default), and transports the momentum when it resamples the projection. Its steps are not confined to $\mathrm{im}\,P_t$, so Prop IV.2 does not apply. Like LDAdam, it is an example of optimizer-state transport.

**Proposition IV.3 (integrability).** [Prop; Frobenius] Let $D$ be a smooth distribution of constant rank $k$ on an open $U\subseteq\Theta$, for instance the anchor distribution $D_\theta=\mathrm{im}\,a(\theta)$ of a time-independent backward method. Then $D$ is involutive on $U$ iff through every $\theta\in U$ passes an injective immersion $\rho_\theta:(\mathbb R^k,0)\to(U,\theta)$ with $\mathrm{im}\,D\rho_\theta=D$ along its image. A single such $\rho$ through $\theta_0$ gives involutivity only along $\mathrm{im}\,\rho$. For example, $D=\mathrm{span}\{\partial_x,\ \partial_y+xz\,\partial_z\}$ on $\mathbb R^3$ has $[\partial_x,\partial_y+xz\partial_z]=z\partial_z\notin D$ off $z=0$, yet the plane $z=0$ is an integral manifold.

The dynamic content needs separate hypotheses.
- In continuous time, if $D$ is involutive, a backward flow with $\dot\theta\in D_\theta$ stays on the leaf through $\theta_0$.
- In the discrete update of Def IV.1, the iterates stay on a leaf for all step sizes and all optimizer outputs $s$ (all losses) iff that leaf contains $(\theta+D_\theta)\cap U$ at every iterate, i.e. the leaves are affine; a constant anchor over the period gives this (Prop IV.2).
- Under gradient flow, dynamic equivalence with a forward lens $(\rho,\mathsf R[\rho])$ forces matching frames, $a_tc_t=D\rho\,\Lambda\,D\rho^\top$ along the trajectory. Hence $K=ac$ is symmetric PSD and, for injective $a$, $c=Ma^\top$ with $M$ symmetric PSD on $S$, possibly depending on $\theta$. If in addition $\rho$ is an immersion with $\dim Q=\operatorname{rank}D$, then $\rho$ is a local isometry from $(Q,\Lambda^{-1})$ onto the leaf with metric $(aMa^\top)^{-1}|_D$, so that metric must be flat. Forward methods with gauge fibres escape this requirement: $\rho(B)=BB^\top$ on symmetric matrices induces the curved Bures–Wasserstein metric, and balanced full-rank LoRA also induces a curved leaf metric. For discrete steps, exact agreement for every step size further forces $\rho$ to be affine along each horizontal line $q+t\,\Lambda D\rho_q^\top g$.

*Instances.* Every projector-type backward method listed in Def IV.1 (GaLore with any $T$ and any projector, LISA, MeZO) has an anchor that does not depend on $\theta$ within its period. Its distribution is constant, hence trivially involutive with affine leaves (vacuously at $T=1$, where a period is a single step). Under the optimizer hypotheses of Prop IV.2 (no weight decay of either kind, no full-gradient clipping; GaLore and MeZO need weight decay $0$, and LISA's AdamW with positive decay violates them), these methods are rebasing schemes of linear forward methods with period $T$, including $T=1$: each MeZO step is a step of the linear method $\theta_t+\lambda z_t$, up to its SPSA estimator, and GaLore with $T=1$ is one-sided LoRA re-based every step. What separates them from LoRA-type methods is the resampling schedule plus the carry-over of optimizer state, not integrability. Flora as published (Adafactor base, with a full-rank current-gradient term in the code), APOLLO (a low-rank-estimated scaling applied to the full gradient) and Fira (a scaled full-rank residual) are not of the form of Def IV.1, because their updates leave $\mathrm{im}\,a$. Non-involutivity proper appears only for $\theta$-dependent anchors. Full-batch GaLore with $T=1$, read as the distribution $D_W=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb R^n,U_r(\nabla L(W)))$ (or $\mathrm{Hom}(V_r(\nabla L(W)),\mathbb R^m)$ when $m\ge n$, as GaLore projects) wherever a singular-value gap exists, is involutive iff $U_r(\nabla L(W))$ (respectively $V_r(\nabla L(W))$) is invariant along $D$. That fails generically for every $r\ge1$ once both matrix dimensions are at least $2$. For every $r$ with $1\le r<\min(m,n)$ it fails for $L=\tfrac12\lVert W\rVert_F^2$, and numerically it fails for least squares and cross-entropy with several samples. It holds, for instance, for single-sample least squares, and automatically only when the distribution is a line field ($nr=1$). Minibatch GaLore with $T=1$ and MeZO violate time-independence, because their anchors depend on step-wise randomness.

*Proof.* Frobenius: if $D$ is involutive, a flat chart $\varphi$ centred at $\theta$, in which $D$ is spanned by the first $k$ coordinate fields, gives $\rho_\theta(y)=\varphi^{-1}(\psi(y),0)$ with $\psi:\mathbb R^k\to(-\delta,\delta)^k$ a diffeomorphism; conversely an injective immersion is locally an embedding, so its local pieces are integral manifolds, along which brackets of sections of $D$ stay in $D$. The same argument along $\mathrm{im}\,\rho$ alone gives involutivity only there, as the example shows. Continuous time: in a flat chart a curve with velocity in $D$ keeps its transverse coordinates constant, so it stays in one slice, and the set of times at which it lies on the leaf through $\theta_0$ is open and closed. Discrete statement: as $\eta$ and $s$ vary, $\theta-\eta a(\theta)s$ ranges over $(\theta+D_\theta)\cap U$, so the iterates stay on the leaf for all $\eta,s$ iff the leaf contains $(\theta+D_\theta)\cap U$. If this holds at every point, a small disc of $\theta+D_\theta$ lies in the leaf; in a flat chart the leaf meets the chart in countably many slices, so the transverse coordinates are constant on the connected disc, and by invariance of domain the disc is open in the slice through $\theta$. Hence $D$ is locally constant along each leaf, so constant on it, and the leaves are affine. A constant anchor gives the planes $\theta+V$. Frames: switching at a time $t_1$ from $L$ to $L+\langle\gamma,\cdot\rangle$, for every covector $\gamma$, gives $a_{t_1}c_{t_1}=D\rho\Lambda D\rho^\top$, which is symmetric PSD with range in $D$; for injective $a$, $M=a^+(ac)(a^+)^\top$ gives $ac=aMa^\top$ and $c=Ma^\top$. If $D\rho_q$ is an isomorphism onto $D$, $D\rho\Lambda D\rho^\top$ is the cometric of the pushforward of the flat metric $\Lambda^{-1}$, so $\rho$ is a local isometry onto the leaf, whose metric must then be flat. For $\rho(B)=BB^\top$, $D\rho D\rho^\top(G)=2(G\theta+\theta G)$ does not depend on the fibre point, and its metric is a constant multiple of the Bures–Wasserstein metric, which is curved for $n\ge2$. Balanced full-rank LoRA ($m=n=r\ge2$, $s=1$, $\eta_A=\eta_B=1$, $B^\top B=AA^\top$, which gradient flow preserves by Thm III.5): $A^\top A=\lvert W\rvert$ and $BB^\top=\lvert W^\top\rvert$, so $K_W(G)=G\lvert W\rvert+\lvert W^\top\rvert G$ depends on $W$ alone. The transpose is an isometry of the leaf metric $\langle K_W^{-1}X,X\rangle$; its fixed positive definite matrices form a totally geodesic submanifold, since an isometry maps geodesics to geodesics, and its induced metric is a constant multiple of the Bures–Wasserstein metric, which has a plane of positive sectional curvature. By the Gauss equation the leaf metric has the same sectional curvature on that plane, so it is not flat (Lee 2018, Chs. 4, 5 and 8; Kobayashi 1958). Discrete steps: agreement of $\rho(q-\eta v)$, $v=\Lambda D\rho_q^\top g$, with $\rho(q)-\eta D\rho_qv$ for every $\eta>0$ and for $\pm g$ makes $\rho$ affine along $q+tv$. GaLore field: where $\sigma_r(\nabla L)>\sigma_{r+1}(\nabla L)$, the projector $\Pi(W)$ onto $U_r(\nabla L(W))$ is a Riesz projector of $\nabla L\nabla L^\top$, smooth because the spectral gap persists (Kato 1995). With $N(Z)=(I-\Pi)\Pi'(Z)\Pi$ one has $\Pi'(Z)=N(Z)+N(Z)^\top$, and the normal part of $[X_E,X_F]$ for $X_E=\Pi E$ is $N(\Pi E)\Pi F-N(\Pi F)\Pi E$. For $n\ge2$ this vanishes for all $E,F$ iff $N=0$ on $D$, that is, iff $U_r$ is invariant along $D$; a line field ($nr=1$) is always involutive. For $L=\tfrac12\lVert W\rVert_F^2$ and $W=\sum_{i\le p}\sigma_ie_ie_i^\top$ with $\sigma_1>\dots>\sigma_p>0$, $p=\min(m,n)$, one finds $N(e_1x^\top)=\sum_{r<j\le p}\frac{\sigma_jx_j}{\sigma_1^2-\sigma_j^2}e_je_1^\top$, so $E=e_1e_{r+1}^\top$ and $F=e_1e_1^\top$ give the normal component $\frac{\sigma_{r+1}}{\sigma_1^2-\sigma_{r+1}^2}e_{r+1}e_1^\top\ne0$ for every $1\le r<p$. For least squares the normal component is real-analytic in the data and not identically zero, so it is nonzero generically; for a single sample, $U_1=\mathrm{span}\{Wx-y\}$ is constant along $D$. For $r=1$ with frame $X_v=u(W)v^\top$, $[X_{v_1},X_{v_2}]=(Mv_1)v_2^\top-(Mv_2)v_1^\top$ with $Mv=Du(W)[uv^\top]\perp u$, which lies in $D$ iff $M=0$. ∎ [Num: for full-batch $T=1$ GaLore the relative normal part of the bracket is $0.08$ to $0.94$ at $r=1$, $(m,n)\in\{(4,3),(5,4)\}$, for least-squares, tanh and cross-entropy losses (referee replication), and $0.21$ to $0.95$ at $(m,n,r)=(4,3,2)$; at $W=\mathrm{diag}(2,1)$ with $L=\tfrac12\|W\|_F^2$ the bracket is $-\tfrac13e_2e_1^\top\notin D$; the cometric of $BB^\top$ takes the same value at $B$ and at $BR$, $R\in O(2)$, to $10^{-12}$.]

*What this explains.* The lens 2-functor separates tangent-side from cotangent-side methods. Frobenius integrability is the test for when a $\theta$-dependent anchor foliates weight space by images of forward methods. For the PEFT zoo it does no discriminating work. Every cotangent method whose update lies in $\mathrm{im}\,a$, whose optimizer does not read the parameter, and which has no decoupled decay on $W$ (GaLore with any projector and MeZO at weight decay $0$, LISA without decay) is, period by period, a forward linear method on a moving base (Exposé V). At $T=1$ this reading is automatic.

**Observation IV.4 (backward reach and the backprop cone).** [Obs; an upper bound] Assume reverse mode with per-box residuals only (no inter-box checkpointing), with boxes taken at the granularity where adapters are separate boxes (for LoRA-FA, $A_0$ and $B$ separately). Then it suffices to keep a VJP residual, meaning an input, an output, a sufficient statistic such as a ReLU mask, or a recipe to recompute one of these inside the box, for
- each trainable box whose parameter-VJP depends on its input: linear and multiplicative boxes such as $W$, $A$, $B$ and (IA)$^3$ scales; additive boxes such as biases and prompts need nothing;
- each *nonlinear* box, bilinear boxes such as the attention products included, on a directed path from a trainable box to the loss.

Deterministic frozen linear boxes, and all boxes off this cone, need nothing; stochastic linear boxes on the cone (dropout) need their mask or RNG state. The $k$ coordinates of $P^\top\nabla L$ can be computed exactly by $k$ forward-mode JVPs, with no reverse pass and $O(1)$-layer activation memory, each JVP costing a small constant multiple (about $1.5$ to $3$) of a forward pass. This always uses less activation memory than un-checkpointed reverse mode, but it is time-competitive with one VJP (about $3$ forward passes) only for $k$ of about $1$ or $2$. It also needs $P$ before the passes, so it cannot produce GaLore's SVD refresh, where $P$ depends on $\nabla L$, more cheaply than a backward pass ($\nabla L$ itself would take $\lvert\theta\rvert$ JVPs).

*Proof.* Cotangents are needed only on the downstream cone of the trainable boxes, so everything else can be pruned. A frozen linear box has $\mathsf R[f](x,y)=W^\top y$, which does not depend on $x$, whereas a nonlinear box's reverse derivative depends on its input through some residual. The parameter-VJP of a trainable linear box is $yx^\top$ (of a multiplicative scale, $\sum y\odot x$), so it needs the input $x$; a bias's parameter cotangent is its incoming cotangent; a dropout box's VJP is $\mathrm{mask}\odot y/(1-p)$. The $i$-th coordinate of $P^\top\nabla L$ is the directional derivative $DL(\theta)[p_i]$, and forward-mode AD computes it alongside one forward pass with a constant-factor overhead and $O(1)$-layer memory. MeZO's central difference has bias $O(\varepsilon^2)$ when $L$ has a Lipschitz Hessian. ∎

*What this explains.* Activation memory across the zoo:
- **LST.** The side network reads the backbone through trainable down-projection ladders, so the backbone's backward pass never runs.
- **LoRA-FA.** With `lora_dropout=0` (the HF default) it stores $A_0x$ ($r$ numbers) instead of $x$ ($n$ numbers); with dropout on, the $n$-element mask is stored too. The saving is realised only where no neighbouring nonlinear box already keeps $x$.
- **Top-layer adaptation** truncates the cone, under the no-checkpointing assumption: with HF's reentrant gradient checkpointing, PEFT calls `enable_input_require_grads()`, so cotangents reach the embeddings. **LISA** trains the embedding and the LM head at every step, so its cone is the whole network and it gets no truncation. On the cone, the unsampled layers' frozen linear boxes drop their input residuals (about $20\%$ of a block's activations at LLaMA proportions without FlashAttention), and relative to LoRA it also drops the adapter residuals, in line with the LISA paper's report of less activation memory than LoRA. Most of its saving against full FT is in gradients and optimizer state. A variant that froze the embedding would truncate the cone at its lowest sampled layer.
- **Prompt tuning** has a small $Q$, comparable to VeRA, FourierFT or (IA)$^3$, and the maximal cone (the whole network), shared with every method that trains something at layer $1$; its prompt tokens also enlarge every stored activation.
- **MeZO** is the case $k=1$, with the JVP estimated by a central difference and no reverse pass. Its estimator is unbiased for the gradient of the Gaussian-smoothed loss and has $O(\varepsilon^2)$ bias relative to $\nabla L$; exact unbiasedness, $\mathbb E[(\nabla L\cdot z)z]=\nabla L$, holds for the exact-JVP forward gradient. GaLore, Flora and LISA all take the reverse route; MeZO's $O(\varepsilon^2)$ bias assumes a Lipschitz Hessian, which ReLU networks lack.

---

## Exposé V. Base change

**Definition V.1 (base change and rebasing).** [Def]

(a) A *function-preserving* pointed reparametrisation $\phi:(\Theta',f',\theta_0')\to(\Theta,f,\theta_0)$, with $f\circ(\phi\times X)=f'$, induces $\phi_!:\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta',\theta_0')\to\mathsf{Meth}(\Theta,\theta_0)$ by postcomposition.

(b) A map $\kappa:\Theta\to\Theta$ that does *not* preserve the function, such as a quantiser, moves the base point. Its **pointing defect** is $e=\kappa\theta_0-\theta_0$.

(c) A **rebasing scheme** (merge-and-restart) picks $\theta_{k+1}\in\mathrm{Im}\,M(\theta_k)$. It has reachable sets $R_K$ and $R_\infty=\bigcup_KR_K$.

**Proposition V.2 (quantisation and splitting).** [Prop] Write $W$ for the full-precision weight, $\kappa$ for the quantiser, $e=\kappa W-W$, and $\delta_0=\delta(q_0)$.

(a) Additive families commute with translation: $M(\theta+v)=\tau_v\circ M(\theta)$. Hence QLoRA is LoRA pointed at $\kappa\theta_0$, with defect $e$; it needs no new weight-space structure. Since $-e$ is generically of full rank, $\theta_0\notin\mathrm{Im}\,\mathrm{LoRA}_r(\kappa\theta_0)$ unless $\operatorname{rank}e\le r$.

(b) For splitting families derived from an SVD or from data, the order of operations matters, and three orders occur in practice:
- *split, then quantise the residual* (QPiSSA; HF's recommended PiSSA/CorDA workflow; the quantisation half-step of LoftQ for $t\ge2$, with $W_{\rm res}=W-\delta(q_{t-1})$): defect $\kappa(W_{\rm res})-W_{\rm res}$;
- *quantise, then fit the error* (LoftQ with one iteration, HF's default `loftq_iter=1`; HF `replace_lora_weights_loftq`): HF sets the factors from $\mathrm{SVD}_r(W-\kappa W)$ without dividing by the LoRA scale $s$ ($\alpha/r$, or $\alpha/\sqrt r$ under rsLoRA), unlike its PiSSA and OLoRA inits, so $\delta_0=-s\,\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)$ and the defect is $e-s\,\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)$, with $\|e-s\,\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)\|^2=\|e-\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)\|^2+(1-s)^2\|\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)\|^2$. It is $e$ minus its best rank-$r$ approximation (rank $\operatorname{rank}e-r$) iff $s=1$, HF's bare default $r=\alpha=8$; for $s=2$ its norm equals $\|e\|$, no better than QLoRA. With a callback, `replace_lora_weights_loftq` can roll a module back to the zero init, whose defect is $e$;
- *quantise, split, re-quantise* (HF `olora_init` on bitsandbytes weights): defect $e+\kappa(r')-r'$ with $r'=\kappa W-\delta_0$.

Quantise-then-split has defect exactly $e$ iff the frozen part equals $\kappa W-\delta_0$, for instance $\kappa W$ kept quantised minus a frozen copy of the rank-$r$ $\delta_0$, at a cost of about $r(m+n)$ extra numbers. Re-quantising the residual, as HF `olora_init` does, generically adds $\kappa(r')-r'$.

Action families whose pointing does not depend on the base (OFT at $R=I$, BOFT at $(R,s)=(I,\mathbf 1)$, HiRA at $B=0$), evaluated at $\kappa\theta_0$, simply carry the defect $e$. Methods whose pointing is computed from the base have an order ambiguity of their own: DoRA's magnitude initialised from $\kappa\theta_0$ gives defect $e$, while initialised from $\theta_0$ it gives $\mathrm{diag}(\|w_{0,i}\|/\|(\kappa\theta_0)_i\|)\,\kappa\theta_0-\theta_0$. For action families a base change also moves the absolute invariants, not only the base point: NF4's exact zero code enlarges the zero set that HiRA must preserve, and exactly orthogonal (exact-Cayley) OFT preserves the Gram matrix of $\kappa\theta_0$ rather than that of $\theta_0$; HF's default Cayley–Neumann OFT preserves it only approximately.

(c) LoftQ alternates two updates on $\|\theta_0-Q-LR\|$: an exact rank-$r$ SVD step, and a round-to-nearest quantisation step with blockwise scales recomputed from its input. The second is not an exact projection, so the objective need not decrease monotonically. With $s=1$ the output is pointed up to the attained residual: an approximate section, meaning a choice $(Q,q_0)$ with reduced $\|\rho(q_0)-\theta_0\|$. In general HF's deployed defect after $T$ iterations is $(Q_T+L_TR_T-W)+(s-1)L_TR_T$, because HF's internal residual also omits $s$. The official LoftQ checkpoints use $r=64$ and $\alpha=16$, so $s=1/4$, and then the deployed defect can grow with $T$ while the attained residual falls.

(d) For a generic high-precision adapter, "fine-tune then quantise" and "quantise then fine-tune" do not commute. The first lands on the quantisation grid. In the second, the exactly merged weight $\kappa\theta_0+\Delta$ leaves the quantised type. For a uniform fixed-scale INT-$k$ grid, $\kappa\theta_0+\Delta$ stays on the grid iff $\Delta$ lies on the scale lattice (within range), so there the obstruction is the off-grid update. NF4 is non-uniform and not closed under addition, and absmax grids whose scales are recomputed are not closed either, so for QLoRA's NF4 the grid itself is part of the obstruction. Type-preserving merges, such as HF PEFT's dequantise–add–requantise for bitsandbytes 4/8-bit layers, re-quantise with recomputed block scales, so they stay in the quantised type but are lossy. Under affine group quantisation QA-LoRA is the structured exception: its group-pooled adapter is constant on each quantisation group and merges into the zero-points, so the merged model stays INT-$k$.

*Proof.* (a) is the definition of additivity. (b) Compute $\rho(q_0)-W$ in each pipeline: $\kappa(W_{\rm res})+\delta_0-W$ with $W=W_{\rm res}+\delta_0$; $\kappa W-s\,\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)-W$, whose norm follows from the Frobenius orthogonality of $\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)$ and $e-\mathrm{SVD}_r(e)$; and $\kappa(r')+\delta_0-W$. For DoRA, $\rho(q_0)=\mathrm{diag}(\mu_0/\|(\kappa\theta_0)_i\|)\,\kappa\theta_0$. (c) By construction; the failure of monotonicity is observed numerically. (d) In general $\kappa(\theta_0+\Delta)\ne\kappa\theta_0+\Delta$; a group-constant $\Delta$ is absorbed into the affine zero-points. ∎ [Num: on a $256\times256$ NF4 example whose weight has a dominant low-rank part, with $r=8$, $\|e\|=5.65$, QPiSSA's defect is $1.45$, LoftQ's ($T=1$, $s=1$) is $5.22$, and the LoftQ objective rises in $27$ of $60$ half-steps; on a Gaussian weight all three defects are close to $\|e\|$, and with $s=2$ the one-step LoftQ defect equals $\|e\|$ exactly; adding an NF4-representable update to an NF4 weight leaves the NF4 grid in most entries.]

**Theorem V.3 (rebasing closure).** [Thm]

(a) *Additive methods with a fixed shape $C\ni0$*, with frames, masks and compression operators frozen at $\theta_0$ across cycles. $R_K=\theta_0+C^{+K}$, the $K$-fold Minkowski sum, and $R_\infty=\theta_0+\mathrm{Mon}(C)$, the additive monoid generated by $C$. If $C$ is closed under real scalings, $R_\infty=\theta_0+\mathrm{span}\,C$.

(b) *Action methods with $e\in S\subseteq G$*, or $\mathrm{id}\in S\subseteq\mathrm{End}(\Theta)$ for scaling methods. $R_K=S^K\theta_0$, and $R_\infty$ is the orbit of the generated monoid. Suppose $S$ lies in the subgroup generated by connected Lie subgroups $H_1,\dots,H_p$ and contains a neighbourhood of $e$ in each. Then the monoid generated by $S$ is the connected Lie subgroup $H$ with $\mathrm{Lie}(H)=\mathrm{Lie}\langle\mathfrak h_1,\dots,\mathfrak h_p\rangle$.

(c) *Block-orthogonal factors.* Let the factors have coordinate blocks $I_j\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}$ (block OFT, BOFT factors, Givens planes). Then $\mathrm{Lie}\langle\mathfrak{so}(I_j)\rangle=\bigoplus_C\mathfrak{so}(C)$, summed over the connected components $C$ of the hypergraph $(\{1..n\},\{I_j\})$.

*Corollaries.*
1. **ReLoRA.** $R_K=W_0+\mathcal M_{\le\min(Kr,m,n)}$. It reaches full fine-tuning iff $K\ge\lceil\min(m,n)/r\rceil$.
2. **Idempotent methods** gain nothing from rebasing: those whose shape is exactly a subspace, subgroup or submonoid independent of the base, with frames, masks and compression operators frozen across cycles. Examples: LoRA-FA with a fixed $A$, LoRA-XS with frozen frames, MoRA with fixed operators, FourierFT with fixed indices, WaveFT, C3A, MiSS (default balance mode; bat mode is base-dependent but still idempotent, since its blockwise maps $W\mapsto(W+I)(I+M)-I$ form a monoid), SHiRA, BitFit, FISH with a fixed mask, LN-tuning, (IA)$^3$, SSF, RoAd. Block OFT with a fixed partition and an exact Cayley map gains only the measure-zero set of block rotations with an eigenvalue $-1$: $R_\infty=W_0\cdot\prod SO(b)=\overline{R_1}$. Constrained OFT (COFT, an $\varepsilon$-ball) with an exact Cayley map gains up to $\prod SO(b)$; HF's `coft=True` keeps the Cayley–Neumann map by default and so falls under corollary 4. Methods that recompute frames or masks from the merged weight (an SVD-recomputing LoRA-XS, ReMoRA's alternating operators, resampled indices) do gain.
3. **VeRA.** $\mathrm{VeRA}^\infty=\text{LoRA-FA}(A)$ whenever the frozen $B$ has no zero entries.
4. **OFT family.** Block OFT with an exact Cayley map is stuck in $W_0\cdot\prod SO(b)$. HF's default Cayley–Neumann factor $R=C(Q)(I-Q^4)$ is not orthogonal: $R^\top R=(I-Q^4)^2$, with singular values $\lvert1-\mu_k^4\rvert$, where $\pm i\mu_k$ are the eigenvalues of $Q$. In the operating regime $\lVert Q\rVert_{\rm op}\le2^{1/4}$ every factor is a contraction, so re-merging decreases $K_{\rm out}$ in the Loewner order and no singular value grows: the drift is monotone shrinkage, and the reachable set lies in $W_0$ times the block contraction semigroup. With unconstrained $Q$ the factors include singular ones ($\mu_k=1$), and re-merging generates $\prod\big(GL^+(b)\cup\mathcal M_{\le b-2}\big)$, or $\prod\big(CO^+(2)\cup\{0\}\big)$ when $b=2$ [Sketch]. Iterated BOFT with $m'$ butterfly factors of block size $b$ reaches $\mathrm{diag}(\mathbb R^m)\cdot W_0\cdot P\big(\prod SO(b\,2^{m'-1})\big)P^\top$, because the factor hypergraph has components of size $b\,2^{m'-1}$. For injective $W_0$ this is $\mathrm{diag}(\mathbb R^m)\cdot W_0\cdot SO(n)$ iff $b\,2^{m'-1}=n$, the full-depth butterfly $m'=1+\log_2(n/b)$. HF's default `boft_n_butterfly_factor=1` is block OFT times $\mathrm{diag}(s)$. A *single* full-depth BOFT has dimension at most $m'\tfrac nb\binom b2<\dim SO(n)$ when $m'(b-1)<n-1$: it is a dense *matrix*, not a dense *set*.
5. **Re-HRA**, with $W_0$ injective, $r$ even and `apply_GS=False`. $R_K=(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le Kr})\cap W_0\cdot SO(n)$: *rebasing commutes with the meet*. This is the multiplicative twin of ReLoRA, and it reaches $W_0\cdot SO(n)$ in at most $\lceil n/r\rceil$ cycles.
6. **GaLore (with a gradient-SVD or a random projector), LISA and MeZO**, under the optimizer hypotheses of Prop IV.2, are rebasing schemes of *linear* methods whose shape moves. That is why, as a set over the sequences of shapes they can choose, they reach all of $\Theta$, while a fixed linear shape $L$ reaches only $\theta_0+L$ (corollary 2). Low rank is the per-period obstruction only for GaLore; LISA and MeZO already make full-rank per-matrix updates and gain coverage. Flora proper takes full-space steps with a compressed momentum, so it has no per-period low-rank shape.
7. **Merging never rescues an exactly orthogonal method from its absolute invariants.** In exact arithmetic, every exactly orthogonal input-side rebasing preserves $K_{\rm out}$ and the spectrum, and every output-side one preserves $K_{\rm in}$ and the spectrum (Cor II.6).

*Proof.* (a) follows by induction. If $C=\mathbb RC$, the monoid generated by $C$ contains $-C$ and is closed under scaling, so it is the span.

(b) Any neighbourhood of $e$ contains a symmetric one, and a symmetric neighbourhood of $e$ in a connected group generates that group as a monoid. The subgroup generated by connected Lie subgroups is the connected Lie subgroup with the generated Lie algebra (Yamabe).

(c) Write $L_{ab}=E_{ab}-E_{ba}$. Then $[L_{ac},L_{cb}]=L_{ab}$ for distinct $a,b,c$. Overlapping hyperedges therefore generate $\mathfrak{so}$ of their union, and $\bigoplus_C\mathfrak{so}(C)$ is a subalgebra that contains every generator.

The corollaries:
- (1) Rank is subadditive, and an SVD splits into blocks.
- (2) The exact Cayley image is dense and open in $SO(b)$, and every element of $SO(b)$ is a product of two elements of it.
- (3) The entries of $\Lambda_bB\Lambda_d$ are $b_iB_{ij}d_j$. Their span over $(b,d)$ is all of $\mathbb R^{m\times r}$ when every $B_{ij}\ne0$ (take $b=e_i$, $d=e_j$).
- (4) Apply (c) to the butterfly blocks; the "only if" of the full-depth equality uses that $W_0$ is injective, as the statement assumes (for $W_0=0$ both sets are $\{0\}$). For the Cayley–Neumann factors, $C(Q)$ is orthogonal and commutes with $Q^4$, so the singular values of $R$ are $\lvert1-\mu_k^4\rvert\le1$ when $\lVert Q\rVert_{\rm op}\le2^{1/4}$, and $\det R=\prod_k(1-\mu_k^4)^2\ge0$. A product of contractions is a contraction, and $WR^\top RW^\top\preceq WW^\top$. The generated Lie algebra is $\mathfrak{gl}(b)$, but that identifies only the generated group: (b) does not apply, since the factors form a $\binom b2$-dimensional set containing no neighbourhood of $e$ in any connected subgroup. For the monoid, $R(Q)R(-Q)=(I-Q^4)^2$ gives positive pair-scalings, $R(Q)\lvert I-Q^4\rvert^{-1}$ gives $SO(b)$, the $KAK$ decomposition then gives $GL^+(b)$, and singular factors give $\mathcal M_{\le b-2}$.
- (5) "$\subseteq$" holds because $R_1R_2-I=R_1(R_2-I)+(R_1-I)$, so ranks add. "$\supseteq$": an $R\in SO(n)$ with $k=\operatorname{rank}(R-I)\le Kr$ is a product of exactly $k$ reflections, with $k$ even (Cartan–Dieudonné–Scherk). Split them into $K$ consecutive groups of even size at most $r$, pad each group with pairs $H_uH_u$, and observe that each group is an HRA$_r$ element.
- The remaining corollaries are immediate. ∎ [Num: for the butterfly hypergraph at $n=64$, $b=4$, the components have sizes $4,8,16,32,64$ for $m'=1,\dots,5$, so it is connected only at $m'=5$ (the referees found the same with HF's own permutation code); the Cayley–Neumann Lie algebra has dimension $16=\dim\mathfrak{gl}(4)$; $K_{\rm out}$ moves by $0.2\%$, $1.3\%$, $6\%$ and $22\%$ (relative Frobenius change, averaged over draws) after $1$, $10$, $50$ and $200$ default-OFT merges with block size $8$ and fresh skew generators with entries of size about $0.05$ in every cycle, decreasing in the Loewner order at every merge; random skew $Q$ with $\lVert Q\rVert_{\rm op}\le2^{1/4}$ never give $\lVert R\rVert_{\rm op}>1$; at $b=2$, $Q=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\1&0\end{pmatrix}$ gives $R=0$.]

*What this explains.* ReLoRA "walks the group its cone generates". Exact-Cayley block OFT can never escape its $n/b$ partial-Gram invariants however often it is re-merged. Butterfly permutations exist precisely to connect the block hypergraph, and they connect it fully only at full depth. **[Pred]** (about reach, not trained performance) With an exact Cayley map and a fixed partition, re-merging adds nothing to block OFT's reach beyond closure; enlarging the reach needs a connected block hypergraph (changing partitions or full-depth butterflies) or a COFT ball. LoRA-type cones gain from rebasing with a fixed shape, while linear additive shapes do not (corollary 2).

**Proposition V.4 (combination rules: gauge and naturality).** [Prop]

(a) A rule $\Phi((B_i,A_i)_i)$ for combining LoRA factors descends to a function of the adapters $(B_iA_i)_i$ iff it is constant on the fibres of $(B_i,A_i)\mapsto(B_iA_i)$. On the full-rank locus this is equivalent to $\prod_iGL_r$-invariance. It is also equivalent for $\Phi$ continuous on the whole factor space; this clause rests on the unique closed orbit in each fibre, which lies in the closure of every orbit of the fibre.
- Task arithmetic $\sum w_is_iB_iA_i$ ($s_i$ is HF's `scaling`, $\alpha_i/r_i$, or $\alpha_i/\sqrt{r_i}$ under rsLoRA; HF's `cat` with merged scale $1$, Obs I.7) is invariant and lands in $\mathrm{LoRA}_{\sum_ir_i}$.
- LoraHub's factor-wise rule $(\sum w_iB_i)(\sum w_jA_j)$ is not invariant. Replacing $(B_2,A_2)$ by $(cB_2,A_2/c)$ changes it by $\sum_{l\ne2}w_lw_2\big[(c-1)B_2A_l+(c^{-1}-1)B_lA_2\big]$. HF's `add_weighted_adapter(..., combination_type="linear")` is a factor-wise rule of the same type: it weights $A_i$ by $\mathrm{sign}(w_i)\sqrt{\lvert w_is_i\rvert}$ and $B_i$ by $\sqrt{\lvert w_is_i\rvert}$ and sums the factors separately, so it has cross terms. On adapters that share a frozen $A$ it is still not exact up to a global factor, since the relative weights become $\sqrt{\lvert w_is_i\rvert}$. HF's non-SVD `ties`, `dare_*` and `magnitude_prune` variants also act on factors; the `*_svd` variants act on full updates.
- Poly and MHR are different. Their skill inventories are trained jointly through the product-of-sums map, which is invariant under the diagonal $GL_r$. For fixed generic routing with fewer skills than tasks and full-rank factors, this is the whole continuous fibre [Num]. With at least as many skills as tasks, or once the routing is counted as a parameter, there are further symmetries (skill permutations, and re-mixings $\alpha\mapsto\alpha M$ with $M\mathbf 1=\mathbf 1$). Their cross terms are a design choice, not an ill-definedness.

(b) *Well-definedness versus exactness.* A joint gauge fixing makes a factor-wise rule well defined. For rank-preserving product-of-sums rules, exactness for all weights $w$ needs degree one in $w$, which for injective factors forces a shared factor; `cat`, which concatenates and raises the rank, is exact without one. A frozen shared factor (FFA-LoRA; LoRA-FA) gives $(\sum w_iB_i)A=\sum w_iB_iA$ exactly. LoraHub with a shared $A$ still returns $(\sum_jw_j)\sum_iw_iB_iA$, exact only up to the global factor $\sum_jw_j$, since its weights are unnormalised. A per-module SVD canonical form leaves a $(\mathbb Z/2)^r$ of sign flips per module, plus rotations within repeated singular values, and these change the cross terms. What is needed is a *joint* frame that produces a shared factor, as KnOTS's joint re-factorisation of the concatenated updates through a shared $U\Sigma$ does. Federated LoRA shows the difference: FedAvg-LoRA clients start each round from the same broadcast $(B,A)$, so their failure, $\tfrac12(B_1+B_2)\cdot\tfrac12(A_1+A_2)\ne\tfrac12(B_1A_1+B_2A_2)$, is bilinearity, and it persists in a fully fixed gauge.

(c) *Naturality.* Task arithmetic is $GL(\Theta)$-equivariant on updates. DARE is coordinatewise and magnitude-free: $D(m\odot\tau)=m\odot(D\tau)$ for every invertible diagonal $D$ and the same mask $m$. So DARE is exactly equivariant under the torus $T_N$, sign flips included, for each mask, and under permutations in distribution; its distributional group is exactly $\mathrm{Mon}_N$. TIES with global trimming (top-$k$ magnitude, sign election by total mass as in the TIES paper and HF's default `"total"`, disjoint mean) is equivariant under $\mathbb R^\times\cdot B_N$, homotheties times signed permutations, generically (off magnitude ties at the threshold). With HF's `majority_sign_method="frequency"`, count ties are broken to $+1$ and the group drops to $\mathbb R_{>0}\cdot S_N$. Per-tensor trimming changes the group to $\prod_b(\mathbb R^\times\cdot B_{N_b})$, extended by permutations of equal-size blocks. DARE followed by full TIES (with trimming) is $\mathbb R^\times\cdot B_N$-equivariant in distribution; HF's and MergeKit's `dare_ties` has no magnitude trim, so with `"total"` election it is exactly $T_N$-equivariant per mask, and its group contains $\mathrm{Mon}_N$. None of the sparsifying rules (DARE, TIES, DARE-TIES) is $O(N)$-equivariant. Applied to factors and summed across adapters (HF's non-SVD variants), all of them are gauge-dependent: under independent per-adapter gauges even DARE fails, through the cross terms of (a). DARE's masking commutes with $T_r$ exactly for each mask and with $\mathrm{Mon}_r$ in distribution, so a single masked adapter, or a factor-wise sum under one gauge shared by all adapters, is $T_r$-invariant per mask and $\mathrm{Mon}_r$-invariant in distribution.

*Proof.* (a) On the full-rank locus, the fibres of $(B_i,A_i)\mapsto(B_iA_i)$ are $\prod GL_r$-orbits (Thm II.2). For $\Phi$ continuous on the whole factor space: fix $W$ of rank $k\le r$. The pairs with $BA=W$ and $\operatorname{rank}B=\operatorname{rank}A=k$ form one $GL_r$-orbit $O_W$, which is closed, and $O_W$ lies in the closure of every orbit of the fibre $\{BA=W\}$. Indeed, split $\mathbb R^r=C_1\oplus C_2\oplus C_3\oplus C_4$ with $\mathrm{im}\,A=C_1\oplus C_2$, $\ker B=C_2\oplus C_3$ and $\dim C_1=k$, and let $g_t$ act by $1$, $t$, $1$, $t^{-1}$ on the four summands; as $t\to0$, $(Bg_t^{-1},g_tA)$ converges to a pair in the fibre whose factors have rank at most $k$, that is, to a point of $O_W$ (the real form of the closed-orbit theorem for reductive quotients; Goodman–Wallach 2009, Ch. 5; Mumford–Fogarty–Kirwan 1994, Ch. 1). Applying this adapter by adapter, two points $x,x'$ of one fibre have gauge paths with $g(t)\cdot x\to o$ and $g'(t)\cdot x'\to o'=h\cdot o$, so invariance and continuity give $\Phi(x)=\Phi(o)=\Phi(o')=\Phi(x')$. Off the full-rank locus, invariance alone is not enough: the projector onto $\mathrm{row}\,A$ is $GL_r$-invariant on $\{\operatorname{rank}A=r\}$, yet it differs at $(0,A)$ and $(0,A')$, which have the same product. Expand the LoraHub product. For Poly/MHR, if $\mathrm{col}\,B_i\cap\mathrm{col}\,B_j=0$ and the routing mixes skills $i$ and $j$, invariance of the cross term under per-skill gauges $g_i,g_j$ forces $B_i(g_i^{-1}g_j-I)A_j=0$, hence $g_i=g_j$.

(b) Flipping column $j$ of $B_i$ together with row $j$ of $A_i$ preserves the SVD form, and changes $B_iA_l$ for $l\ne i$.

(c) Masks and the rescaling by $1/(1-p)$ commute with diagonal maps, and an i.i.d. mask is permutation-invariant in law. Trimming by magnitude, electing signs by total mass and taking disjoint means commute with signed coordinate permutations and with $c\cdot I$ for $c\ne0$; with count-based election, ties have positive probability and are broken to $+1$, which is not sign-symmetric. Per-tensor trimming commutes with an independent homothety on each block. A rotation changes the magnitudes of individual coordinates. On factors, the all-ones mask has positive probability, and on it factor-wise DARE is the factor-wise rule of (a) times $(1-p)^{-2}$, so a per-adapter torus gauge changes it. ∎ [Num: DARE with a random non-unit diagonal $D$ is equivariant to $2\times10^{-15}$ per mask; TIES is homogeneous to $9\times10^{-16}$ and fails by $5.6$ under a generic positive diagonal; the general LoraHub change formula matches at $N=3$ to $2\times10^{-15}$; factor-wise DARE changes by $O(1)$ under a diagonal gauge on one adapter and by $10^{-15}$ under one shared by all adapters; for Poly with $3$ skills, $r=2$ and $6\times6$ weights, the fibre has dimension $32$ with $2$ tasks and $4=\dim GL_2$ with $4$ tasks.]

*What this explains.* Post-hoc factor-space merging of independently trained modules (LoraHub, HF's `linear`) is not a function of the adapters. FFA-LoRA repairs federated LoRA's bilinearity. KnOTS's joint re-factorisation produces a shared factor. **Design rule:** combine LoRAs on products, or only after a joint re-factorisation that produces a shared factor. **[Pred]** LoraHub behaves like update arithmetic, up to the factor $\sum_jw_j$, when its modules share the $A$-seed and $A$ stays close to its initialisation (an empirical regime, favoured by large $\eta_B/\eta_A$ as in LoRA+); it degrades when the modules come from independent seeds.

---

## Exposé VI. Extensions and mergeability

**Definition VI.1 (extensions; mergeability).** [Def]
- An **extension** is a function-preserving pointed embedding $\iota:(\Theta,f,\theta_0)\to(\Theta\times\Theta',\tilde f,(\theta_0,\theta_0'))$, $\iota(\theta)=(\theta,\theta_0')$, with $\tilde f(\theta,\theta_0',-)=f(\theta,-)$. The point $\theta_0'$ is the **neutral point** ("identity at initialisation").
- An **unpointed extension** has no neutral point.
- A method on an extension is **mergeable** if there is $\mu:Q\to\Theta$ with $\Phi_{\tilde f}\circ\rho=\Phi_f\circ\mu$: a lift along the realisation.
- It is **box-locally mergeable** if the modified box function stays in the box's function class: linear maps for a linear box, affine maps when the box has a bias slot. Thm VI.3 gives the side conditions (position-uniformity, no weight sharing) under which this yields a merge into the base network.

**Lemma VI.2 (the activation fixes the privileged basis).** [Lemma; classical: Sussmann 1992; Chen–Lu–Hecht-Nielsen 1993; Godfrey et al. 2022] Let $\sigma:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R$ be an elementwise activation that is not affine, and let $L\in GL_d$ commute with it as maps on $\mathbb R^d$: $L\,\sigma^{\times d}=\sigma^{\times d}L$.

(a) If $\sigma\in C^2$, then $L=PD$ is monomial.

(b) If $\sigma$ is ReLU or leaky ReLU with slope $\alpha\ne1$, then $L=PD$ with $D$ a positive diagonal.

(c) If moreover $\sigma$ is real-analytic at $0$ with $\sigma(t)=\sum a_kt^k$, each diagonal entry $c$ of $D$ satisfies $ca_k=c^ka_k$ for all $k$. So $c=1$ for GELU and SiLU, since $\sigma''(0)\neq0$, and $c=\pm1$ for tanh.

*Proof.* (a) Fix row $i$; its row vector $\ell_i$ is nonzero because $L$ is invertible. The left side $\sum_jL_{ij}\sigma(x_j)$ has vanishing mixed partials $\partial_j\partial_k$ for $j\ne k$. The right side has mixed partial $L_{ij}L_{ik}\sigma''(\ell_i\cdot x)$. Choose $t_0$ with $\sigma''(t_0)\ne0$, which exists because $\sigma$ is not affine, and $x$ with $\ell_i\cdot x=t_0$. So $L_{ij}L_{ik}=0$: each row has a single nonzero entry.

(b) ReLU is not $C^2$, so argue by kinks instead. The left side of row $i$ is non-differentiable exactly on $\bigcup_{j:L_{ij}\ne0}\{x_j=0\}$, and the right side exactly on the hyperplane $\{\ell_i\cdot x=0\}$. The two sets are equal, and a hyperplane equal to a finite union of coordinate hyperplanes is one of them. So row $i$ has a single entry $L_{ij}$, and $L_{ij}\sigma(t)=\sigma(L_{ij}t)$ at $t=1$ forces $L_{ij}>0$. Leaky ReLU is identical.

(c) $P$ commutes with $\sigma^{\times d}$, so $D\sigma=\sigma D$, i.e. $c\,\sigma(t)=\sigma(ct)$; compare Taylor coefficients at $0$. ∎

*What this explains.* Within the lemma's scope (global linear intertwiners of an ungated elementwise $\sigma$), the symmetries a method may ignore are *derived* from $\sigma$. For ungated FFNs (BERT, GPT-2, ViT), GELU and SiLU give $S_d$, tanh gives $B_d$, and ReLU gives positive monomials. Gated FFNs (SwiGLU and GeGLU, as in LLaMA, Mistral, Qwen and Gemma) are not elementwise, and their hidden-unit group is the full $\mathrm{Mon}_{d_{\rm ff}}$, acting through the linear branch (R4 of Thm VI.3). Affine, bias-absorbing symmetries can be larger still: sigmoid satisfies $\sigma(-z)=1-\sigma(z)$. The privileged basis of LayerNorm gains comes from the diagonal gain parametrisation, not from $\sigma$; a gain-free RMSNorm is $O(d)$-equivariant, which SliceGPT and QuaRot exploit. **[Pred]** Over random function-preserving permutations of hidden units, LoRA's outcome variance equals its seed variance, since the pretrained order is exchangeable. FourierFT, LoKr and MoRA show larger variance, and so do BOFT and OFT on modules whose input side is the hidden layer (`down_proj`). On `up_proj` and `gate_proj` a hidden-unit permutation only reorders BOFT's output rows, so BOFT is exactly equivariant there.

**Theorem VI.3 (the merge calculus).** [Thm; the rules are elementary, and the merge facts for (IA)$^3$ (Liu et al. 2022, Sec. 3.3), SSF (re-parameterisation) and LoRA (linearity) are known. Scale migration through SwiGLU, ReLU and norms, and the GELU obstruction, are used by AWQ (Lin et al. 2024) and SmoothQuant (Xiao et al. 2023). New: only the packaging as a merge calculus for PEFT, with explicit side conditions] Work with activation-side diagrams in a symmetric monoidal category of continuous, piecewise-smooth maps with copy and discard nodes. (We call copy and the Hadamard product "spiders" by analogy only; in this cartesian setting they do not satisfy the Frobenius law.) A *trainable box* is a node whose weight ranges over a stated class: all linear maps, or all affine maps when the box has a bias slot. R2 and R4–R7 are identities of maps; R1 is a closure property and R3 a characterisation.

| rule | statement | when it holds |
|---|---|---|
| R1 (fusion) | composites and parallel sums of linear (affine) boxes are linear (affine) | the operators involved are position-independent |
| R2 (permutation slide) | $P\sigma^{\times d}=\sigma^{\times d}P$ | every elementwise $\sigma$ |
| R3 (positive-diagonal slide) | $\mathrm{diag}(\ell)\sigma^{\times d}=\sigma^{\times d}\mathrm{diag}(\ell)$ for all $\ell\ge0$ | iff $\sigma$ is positively homogeneous (ReLU, leaky ReLU; not GELU, SiLU, tanh); such $\sigma$ are piecewise linear |
| R4 (spider slide) | $\mathrm{diag}(\ell)(a\odot b)=(\mathrm{diag}(\ell)a)\odot b$ | any $\ell$ |
| R5 (cup slide) | $\langle Mq,k\rangle=\langle q,M^\top k\rangle$ | always. Under RoPE, $\langle R_mMq,R_nk\rangle=\langle q,M^\top R_{n-m}k\rangle$ holds for every $M$, but the slide onto the key side, $\langle R_mMq,R_nk\rangle=\langle R_mq,R_nM^\top k\rangle$ for all $m,n$, holds iff $M^\top$ commutes with every $R_{n-m}$ that occurs; for diagonal $M$ and distinct frequencies, iff $\ell$ is constant on each rotary pair (in either pairing convention) |
| R6 (norm absorption) | $W(\gamma\odot n(x)+\beta)=(W\mathrm{diag}\gamma)\,n(x)+W\beta$ | always; the shift $W\beta$ needs a bias slot, and an RMSNorm gain has no $\beta$ |
| R7 (copy slide) | applying $D$ and then copying equals copying and then applying $D$ to every copy | always |

Consequences:

(a) **Affine edits.** Let a method insert a position-uniform affine edit $E_q$ on a wire $w$ of the base network $f$, for every $q\in Q$. Suppose every reader of $w$ is a trainable, unshared box (no weight tying) whose class is closed under precomposition with $E_q$; a shift needs a bias slot. Then the method merges, by $W_c\mapsto W_cE_q$ for all readers $c$ at once. Dually, it merges into an untied trainable box that writes $w$ whenever $E_q$ composed with that box stays in its class. Absorption into a box that the extension itself inserts does not count. Edits that violate these hypotheses fall outside (a): on the reader side, R1–R7 do not merge position-selective edits or edits on a wire that is also read by a skip connection, a norm or the network output. Whole-network non-mergeability is open (Exposé XI). Position-selective edits (ReFT/LoReFT, and position-gated LoRA variants such as Activated LoRA) are not box-locally mergeable. Residual-stream edits need case analysis: an edit merges into the box that produces the wire when that box can absorb it (an untied embedding table; for a fixed-position edit at layer $0$, a learned absolute positional table), a shift folds into a writer's bias when one exists, a diagonal edit after a post-LN folds into that LN's affine parameters, and an orthogonal edit in an RMSNorm model merges globally, SliceGPT/QuaRot-style. No merge is known for LoReFT's non-orthogonal low-rank edit.

(b) **Diagonal methods.** A diagonal method merges into a box reached by R1–R7 if all of the following hold: the rules move *every* copy of $\mathrm{diag}(\ell)$ into trainable boxes for all $\ell$ in the method's parameter domain (with $\ell\ge0$ when R3 is used); no position-dependent operator lies on the path (for RoPE, $\ell$ is constant on each rotary pair); the target box is unshared, or $\ell$ is constant across the sharing group; and every shift has a bias slot. Conversely, the RoPE condition is necessary for sliding a query scaling onto $W_K$ when $W_Q$ and $W_K$ have full rank and at least two occurring relative offsets have rotation angles that are not multiples of $\pi$. Without such genericity hypotheses the conditions are not necessary: a dead rotary pair, or zero entries of $\ell$ under ReLU, can still merge.

(c) **Non-affine edits.** For a linear or affine box, an edit whose modified box function is non-affine in the box input, for some parameter value, is not box-locally mergeable. For linear and affine boxes this is the definition; for non-linear boxes such as attention, see Thm VI.5(f).

(d) **Type relativity.** A generic high-precision update is off the quantisation grid, so exact merging of a QLoRA or LoftQ update leaves the quantised type, a type-preserving merge is lossy, and QA-LoRA's group-constant adapters are the exception (Prop V.2(d)).

*Proof.* R2, R4, R5, R6 and R7 are identities; under RoPE, $R_m^\top R_n=R_{n-m}$ gives the first form of R5 for every $M$. For R3, $\ell\sigma(t)=\sigma(\ell t)$ for all $\ell\ge0$ and all $t$ is positive homogeneity of degree one, and a $C^1$ positively homogeneous $\sigma$ on $\mathbb R$ is linear. (a) The closure and reader conditions put $W_cE_q$ in each reader's class, R7 distributes $E_q$ to every reader, and position-uniformity lets one weight absorb the edit at every position. Dually, if $w$ is the output of an untied box $p(x)=W_px+b_p$ and $E_q\circ p$ lies in its class, then for $E_q(x)=A_qx+c_q$ replacing $p$ by $E_q\circ p$ ($W_p\mapsto A_qW_p$, $b_p\mapsto A_qb_p+c_q$) is a merge (R1). Skip connections, norms and the network output carry no trainable weight that could absorb an edit they read, and R1 does not fuse a position-selective edit into a weight that acts at every position. (b) The "if" direction is (a) applied at the box reached. For the RoPE converse, equality of all logits with full-rank $W_Q,W_K$ forces $R_\Delta^{-1}\mathrm{diag}(\ell)R_\Delta$ to be one matrix for every occurring offset $\Delta$, including $\Delta=0$, so $\mathrm{diag}(\ell)$ commutes with $R_\Delta$; on a rotary pair whose angle is not a multiple of $\pi$ this forces equal entries. (c) is the definition of box-local mergeability. (d) is Prop V.2(d). ∎ [Num: under RoPE, $\langle R_mMq,R_nk\rangle=\langle q,M^\top R_{n-m}k\rangle$ holds for a generic $M$ to $10^{-15}$ while the slide onto the key side fails; a query scaling slid onto $W_K$ leaves a $7.6\%$ least-squares residual unless $\ell$ is pair-constant; folding post-LN gains into $W_1$ changes the output; legal rewrites agree to $10^{-15}$.]

*Corollaries.* All weight-space reparametrisation methods are mergeable, because their nonlinearities sit on the parameter side (Cayley in OFT, the norm in DoRA, $u/\|u\|$ in HRA). In HF practice an adapter on one side of a tied embedding/LM head is not merged faithfully (`ensure_weight_tying=False` by default). The scaling methods merge as follows.
- (IA)$^3$ on keys and values is an output scaling of $W_K,W_V$ and fuses into them. On the FFN, HF applies it to the input of $W_{\rm down}$, and it fuses there. Both hold for every $\sigma$.
- Alternatively, the FFN scaling fuses into $W_{\rm up}$ under ReLU when $\ell>0$, and **under SwiGLU/GeGLU for every $\ell$**, by R4 applied to $\mathrm{silu}(W_gx)\odot W_ux$. Under GELU it does **not** fuse into $W_{\rm up}$, except for entries with $\ell_i\in\{0,1\}$: with pre-activations ranging over $\mathbb R$, row $i$ of any fused $W'$ must be $c\,w_i$ (level sets), and $\ell_i\,\mathrm{gelu}(t)=\mathrm{gelu}(ct)$ forces $c=\ell_i$ as $t\to+\infty$ and $c^2=1$ from the Gaussian tail as $t\to-\infty$. The $W_{\rm up}$ statement is practically idle, since $W_{\rm down}$ always works, and AWQ's code already migrates scales this way (into `up_proj` under SwiGLU, into `fc1` under ReLU, and through an inserted scaled activation under GELU).
- A query scaling fuses into $W_Q$ (R1). It moves onto $W_K$ (R5) only without RoPE or with $\ell$ constant on rotary pairs, and only without GQA sharing or with $\ell$ constant across the group.
- SSF fuses into the preceding linear box when that box has a bias slot; in bias-free LLaMA-type layers its shift needs a new bias. LN-tuning edits existing LN parameters, so it is trivially mergeable. Folding a LayerNorm's $\gamma,\beta$ into the following box works in pre-LN blocks when that box has a bias slot for $W\beta$; an RMSNorm gain has no $\beta$ and folds without one (the SliceGPT/QuaRot fold). Neither folds into a tied embedding/LM head without breaking the tie.

Not box-locally mergeable: nonlinear Houlsby, Pfeiffer and parallel adapters; routed mixtures (MoLE, LoRAMoE, X-LoRA), unless the gate is constant; AdapterFusion, even with a constant gate, since it combines nonlinear adapters; LST; prefix-type methods (Thm VI.5).

*What this explains.* Exact merging into existing weights is certified by local rewriting, a sufficient test together with box-local obstructions, under explicit side conditions (position-uniformity, no sharing, bias slots), and the rewrite also tells you *which* weight absorbs the update. It is not a decision procedure for whole-network mergeability. Zero inference latency is a weaker goal (a new bias costs almost nothing), and for quantised bases the type caveat (d) applies.

**Proposition VI.4 (linear insertions, and ReFT).** [Prop]

(a) A linear adapter in parallel with a single linear box, $W_0x+sUDx$, *is* LoRA: it has the same $\rho$ (He et al. 2022, Table 1). A linear adapter parallel to a whole sublayer (He et al.'s parallel adapter around attention or the FFN) edits the weightless skip connection rather than a base box, and is not box-locally mergeable when the sublayer is non-affine.

(b) Let $W_0\in\mathbb R^{m\times n}$ be bias-free (as LLaMA-type $W_o$ and $W_{\rm down}$ are), and $r\ge1$. A serial linear adapter after $W_0$, $(I+UD)W_0$ with $U\in\mathbb R^{m\times r}$ and $D\in\mathbb R^{r\times m}$ (scale absorbed), has image $W_0+\{Y\in\mathcal M_{\le r}:\mathrm{row}\,Y\subseteq\mathrm{row}\,W_0\}$. It is expressively equivalent to LoRA iff $\operatorname{rank}W_0=n$, and weakly (unpointed) isomorphic to LoRA iff $W_0$ is invertible. The isomorphism $(U,D)\mapsto(U,DW_0)$ sends a base point $(U_0,D_0)$ to $(U_0,D_0W_0)$. For a fixed $\mathrm T_A$ pointing $(0,A_0)$ with $\operatorname{rank}A_0=r$ and $1\le r<m=n$, a pointed isomorphism exists iff $W_0$ is invertible and $\mathrm{row}\,A_0=\mathrm{row}(D_0W_0)$, and then $U_0=0$ automatically. With a bias $b_0$, the serial adapter also moves the bias by $UDb_0$, so on the affine box its image differs from weight-only LoRA's. Houlsby's near-identity initialisation (weights from a zero-mean Gaussian with standard deviation $10^{-2}$, truncated at two standard deviations) makes practical serial adapters slightly unpointed; the pointed statement applies to zero-up initialisations.

(c) LoReFT (Wu et al. 2024) is $\Phi(h)=h+R^\top(Wh+b-Rh)$, with $R\in\mathbb R^{r\times d}$ having orthonormal rows, applied at selected positions. On one hidden vector it is the extension by an **identity box** followed by a rank-$\le r$ affine edit,
$$\Phi(h)=(I+R^\top(W-R))h+R^\top b,$$
which is LoRA$_r$ on the *affine* identity box $[I_d\mid0]$ in homogeneous coordinates, with $B=R^\top$ constrained to the Stiefel manifold and $A=[W-R\mid b]$. Its image is $(I,0)+\mathcal M_{\le r}(d\times(d+1))$, of dimension $r(2d+1-r)$. The bias is confined to $\mathrm{col}\,B$, so for $r<d$ this is not LoRA$_r$ $\oplus$ BitFit, whose dimension is larger by $d-r$. It is simulated, through $h(R,W,b)=(R^\top,[W-R\mid b])$, by LoRA$_r$ on that affine box pointed at $(R_0^\top,0)$, a $\mathrm T_B$ pointing with $\mathrm{col}\,B_0=\mathrm{row}\,R_0$; this homogeneous-coordinate LoRA has a bias column in $A$, unlike HF LoRA. It is not simulated by $\mathrm T_A$-pointed LoRA$_r$, since its $\dim S_1=r(d+1)$ exceeds $rd$. It is not isomorphic to LoRA$_r$: its parameter count is smaller by $r(r+1)/2$, and its residual symmetry is $O(r)$, acting by $(R,W,b)\mapsto(OR,OW,Ob)$. It is pointed iff $W=R$ and $b=0$. The reference implementation (pyreft) initialises $W$ and $b$ by the default `nn.Linear` init, independently of $R$, so practical LoReFT has a pointing defect $R^\top((W_0-R_0)h+b_0)$: at initialisation it replaces the $R$-component of $h$ by a random affine function, and the simulation above is then only a weak morphism. pyreft also applies dropout to the whole output, $h$ included, so the training-time map is not $\Phi$.

*Proof.* (a) holds by definition; a sublayer-parallel adapter adds $sUDx$ to $F(x)$ for a non-affine sublayer $F$, and no base weight sees $x$ linearly. (b) $UDW_0$ ranges over the rank-$\le r$ matrices whose rows lie in $\mathrm{row}W_0$, which is all of $\mathcal M_{\le r}$ iff $\mathrm{row}W_0=\mathbb R^n$. If $W_0$ is invertible, $(U,D)\mapsto(U,DW_0)$ is a linear bijection over $\Theta$. Conversely, an isomorphism preserves $\mathrm{Im}$ (Obs I.4(c)), forcing $\operatorname{rank}W_0=n$, and is a diffeomorphism, forcing $2mr=r(m+n)$, i.e. $m=n$. Pointed case, "if": pointedness gives $U_0D_0W_0=0$, so $U_0D_0=0$; $\operatorname{rank}D_0=r$ then forces $U_0=0$, and composing $(U,D)\mapsto(U,DW_0)$ with the gauge $g\in GL_r$ with $A_0=gD_0W_0$ gives the pointed isomorphism. "Only if": the unpointed part gives $W_0$ invertible, and Obs I.6 gives $\{XD_0W_0+U_0Y\}=\{Z:\mathrm{row}\,Z\subseteq\mathrm{row}\,A_0\}$; since $r<n$, a nonzero $U_0$ would produce some $U_0Y$ with a row outside $\mathrm{row}\,A_0$, so $U_0=0$ and the row spaces agree. With a bias, $(I+UD)(W_0x+b_0)=(W_0+UDW_0)x+b_0+UDb_0$. (c) Expand. Every $d\times(d+1)$ matrix of rank $\le r$ factors as $R^\top V$ with $R^\top$ having orthonormal columns (QR), so the image is as stated. $R^\top$ is injective, so the edit vanishes iff $W=R$ and $b=0$. Finally, $R^\top O^\top(OW-OR)=R^\top(W-R)$. The parameter count is $rd-\tfrac{r(r+1)}2+rd+r$ against $r(2d+1)$, and at the neutral point $S_1=\{R_0^\top V\}$ has dimension $r(d+1)$. ∎ [Num: Jacobian rank $22=r(2d+1-r)$ at $d=6$, $r=2$, against $26$ for LoRA$_r$ $\oplus$ bias; serial-adapter image dimension $7$ at $(m,n,r)=(5,3,1)$ and at $(4,8,1)$, against $11$ for LoRA at $(4,8,1)$.]

*What this explains.* Per hidden vector, LoReFT (and DiReFT) is not a new geometric type: its image is that of LoRA$_r$ on an affine identity box the architecture did not have. Wu et al. already describe DiReFT as LoRA applied to hidden representations at certain positions, and name position as what separates ReFT from PEFT; the new content here is the image dimension, the bias confinement, the $O(r)$ gauge and the initialisation defect. ReFT as a framework also allows nonlinear interventions (pyreft's `act_fn`), to which this does not apply. The main obstruction to merging is position-selectivity: LoReFT acts only at selected prompt positions, so no position-independent weight can absorb it. Even applied at every position, at interior residual-stream sites the stream is read by the skip connection and by every later block, and no linear box of the original architecture sits there (at the embedding output an untied embedding table could absorb a position-uniform edit, Thm VI.3(a)). By Def VI.1 it is box-locally mergeable into its own inserted box, which does not help.

**Theorem VI.5 (attention is a homomorphism followed by a perspective map).** [Thm; the identity is standard; (b) is due to He et al. 2022; (c) and the soft-prompt-to-prefix map in (e) are due to Petrov–Torr–Bibi 2024, §2.2 (informally Li–Liang 2021, §7.2); the monoid organisation, the explicit position hypotheses and the slice-category typing of (e) are ours] Fix a query $q$. Multisets of key–value pairs form a commutative monoid under $\uplus$. Define
$$\Sigma_q(K)=\Big(\sum e^{\langle q,k\rangle/\sqrt d},\ \sum e^{\langle q,k\rangle/\sqrt d}\,v\Big).$$
(a) $\Sigma_q$ is a monoid homomorphism into $(\mathbb R_{\ge0}\times\mathbb R^{d_v},+)$, landing in $\{Z>0\}\cup\{(0,0)\}$. On nonempty multisets, $\mathrm{Attn}_q=\pi\circ\Sigma_q$ with the partial map $\pi(Z,S)=S/Z$, defined for $Z>0$.

(b) **Prefix is a gated parallel adapter.** For nonempty $P$ and $C$, $\mathrm{Attn}_q(P\uplus C)=(1-\lambda_q)\mathrm{Attn}_q(C)+\lambda_q\mathrm{Attn}_q(P)$, with $\lambda_q=Z_q(P)/(Z_q(P)+Z_q(C))$.

(c) **Per-layer pattern rigidity.** Within a single layer, for fixed $q$ and a fixed content multiset $C$: the content attention weights are multiplied by $1-\lambda_q$, their ratios are unchanged, and the output lies on the segment from $\mathrm{Attn}_q(C)$ to $\mathrm{Attn}_q(P)\in\mathrm{conv}\,V_P$. This rigidity is relative to the same layer's computation without the prefix; it is relative to the pretrained pattern only at the lowest prefixed layer. Lower-layer prefixes change the queries and the content keys and values of higher layers, so attention patterns at the level of the network are *not* rigid (Petrov–Torr–Bibi 2024, §6; the same authors show that prefix-tuning a pretrained transformer can be a universal approximator, arXiv:2402.14753).

(d) **LLaMA-Adapter** normalises $P$ and $C$ separately and adds a zero-initialised gate: $\mathrm{Attn}_q(C)+\tanh(g_l)\,\mathrm{Attn}_q(P)$, with one gate per head in the paper; HF PEFT uses one raw zero-initialised scalar per layer, without $\tanh$. It uses two perspective maps, leaves the content weights exactly unchanged, and is pointed at $g=0$. Its prompt keys and values are the frozen $W_k,W_v$ applied to one learned prompt per adapted layer, and it acts in the top layers only.

(e) **Prompt $\to$ deep prefix in causal decoders.** Assume that the soft prompt comes first, that attention is causal, and that content positions are offset by the prompt length $m$ in both methods (as HF PEFT does via `cache_position`). Then at every layer the states at prompt positions depend only on $p$, and $h:p\mapsto(K_j(p),V_j(p))_j$ satisfies $\Phi\circ\rho_{\rm prefix}\circ h=\Phi\circ\rho_{\rm prompt}$ as functions of the content tokens, read at content positions. The two methods live on different extensions, so this is an arrow of the unpointed slice over the realisation $C^\infty(X_{\rm content},Y_{\rm content})$, not of $\mathsf{Meth}$. Under RoPE without the offset, a different $h$ works: rotate each prefix key by $-m$ (positions $-m,\dots,-1$). For additive relative biases (ALiBi, T5) the answer depends on how the implementation places the prefix keys. The construction does not apply in bidirectional encoders of depth $\ge2$.

(f) **Obstructions.** Call a head *standard* if it is vanilla softmax attention without torch-style `add_bias_kv` (which would absorb a one-token prefix), `add_zero_attn`, learned attention sinks or softmax-plus-one. On length-one contexts, every weight setting of a standard head is affine in $x$. Some prefix makes the prefixed head non-affine on length-one contexts iff $W_o\ne0$ and ($W_q\ne0$ or $W_k^\top b_q\ne0$); for bias-free heads, iff $W_o\ne0$ and $W_q\ne0$. For such frozen weights and generic prefixes the prefixed head is not affine, because the gate $\lambda(x)$ is not constant. So prefix tuning and P-tuning v2 are not box-locally mergeable into a standard head. When $W_oW_v\ne0$, no prefix is neutral, so prompt and prefix tuning are unpointed.

*Proof.* (a) Sums over a disjoint union split, and $Z=0$ only for the empty multiset.

(b) $\pi(Z_P+Z_C,S_P+S_C)=\lambda S_P/Z_P+(1-\lambda)S_C/Z_C$.

(c) Each content weight is $e^{\langle q,k\rangle/\sqrt d}/(Z_P+Z_C)=(1-\lambda)e^{\langle q,k\rangle/\sqrt d}/Z_C$, and $\mathrm{Attn}_q(P)$ is a convex combination of $V_P$.

(d) This is the definition.

(e) Induction over layers: under causal masking, prompt positions attend only to prompt positions, and with the stated offset their positional encodings agree in both methods. Without the offset under RoPE, prefix keys sit in the cache already rotated, so the content-to-prompt displacement changes unless each key is rotated back by $-m$.

(f) A softmax over one token equals $1$, so the original head computes $W_o(W_vx+b_v)+b_o$. With a prefix, the output is $(1-\lambda(x))W_o(W_vx+b_v)+\lambda(x)W_o\mathrm{Attn}_{q(x)}(P)+b_o$. If $W_o=0$ it is constant. If $W_q=0$ and $W_k^\top b_q=0$, every score is constant in $x$, so $\lambda$ is constant and the output is affine. Otherwise, take a ray $x=tu$ along which some score difference varies (with $W_qu\ne0$, or with the content score $\langle b_q,W_ktu\rangle$ varying), one prefix token whose key keeps the gate $\lambda(t)$ non-constant, and a value $v$ with $W_ov$ large. The gate is a bounded, non-constant analytic function of $t$, so $\lambda''\ne0$ somewhere, and for large $W_ov$ the term $\lambda(t)W_ov$ dominates the second difference. The prefixed output is real-analytic in $(x,\text{prefix})$, so this witness makes the prefixes with affine output a nowhere-dense analytic subset. Unpointedness: $\lambda(x)>0$ always, and a neutral prefix would need $W_o\mathrm{Attn}_{q(x)}(P)=W_o(W_vx+b_v)$ for all $x$; the left side lies in the compact set $W_o\,\mathrm{conv}\,V_P$, while the right side is unbounded when $W_oW_v\ne0$. ∎ [Num: on a 3-layer, 2-head causal decoder, prompt-to-prefix agreement is $9\times10^{-16}$ with RoPE and the offset, against $1.3$ without it and $2\times10^{-15}$ with keys rotated by $-m$; a head with $W_o=0$ stays affine although $W_q^\top W_k\ne0$, and one with $W_q\ne0$, $W_q^\top W_k=0$ does not (referee replication).]

*What this explains.* At the lowest prefixed layer the content attention equals the pretrained one, rescaled by $1-\lambda_q$. At higher layers it changes only through the modified inputs, to first order additively in each position (Petrov–Torr–Bibi 2024, §6), so new query–key interactions cannot be imposed directly at a layer. No network-level impossibility follows. LLaMA-Adapter's zero-init attention is a structural change; categorically, its contribution is *pointedness*. Prompt tuning maps into P-tuning v2 in causal decoders. Petrov et al. and Li–Liang assert that the inclusion is strict; it is strict in a one-layer, one-token example, where a prompt fixes the prefix value as a function of its key, and we do not prove the generic statement. Encoders remain open.

---

## Exposé VII. Worked examples: fourteen methods through the three invariants

Each card gives the object $(Q,q_0,\rho)$ and its invariants: the image germ (I), the fibre and metric (F), base change (B) and merge type (M).

**LoRA.** $\rho=W_0+sBA$, $q_0=(0,A_0)$.
- *I:* the cone $W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r}$, of dimension $r(m+n-r)$, with apex at $\theta_0$ and deficit $r(n-r)$.
- *F:* gauge $GL_r$. With per-block rates, its isometric half is $\mathfrak{so}_r$ and its $\mathrm{Sym}_r$ half carries the charge $\Phi\equiv-A_0A_0^\top/\eta_A$ under gradient flow. SGD is $O(r)$-natural, Adam only $B_r$-natural. Under gradient flow the dynamics cross over at $\sigma^*$.
- *B:* rebasing reaches $\mathcal M_{\le Kr}$. Generic initialisations fall into the three strata $\mathrm{Gr}\sqcup\mathrm{Gr}\sqcup\mathcal M_r$.
- *M:* fuses.

**DoRA.** $\rho=\mathrm{diag}(\mu)N(W_0+sBA)$.
- *I:* $\mathrm{Diag}_m\cdot(W_0+\mathcal M_{\le r})$, the image of LoRA plus a trainable output scale. Its dimension is $\min(mn,\,r(m+n-r)+m)$ for generic $W_0$, and the rank can exceed $r$.
- *F:* gauge $GL_r$ for generic $W_0$ with $(m-r)(n-r)\ge m$. $\Phi$ survives the detached norm. Under gradient flow the exact gradient changes the metric, and the detached backward is a non-gradient update; under AdamW the magnitude coordinates matter too.
- *M:* fuses. Experiment D separates the possible sources of a gap to "LoRA + output scale": parametrisation, backward rule and optimizer coordinates.

**OFT / BOFT.** $W_0R^\top$, with $R$ block-Cayley or a product of butterfly factors; BOFT adds $\mathrm{diag}(s)$.
- *I:* with an exact Cayley map, an orbit in the $K_{\rm out}$ level set; block OFT also fixes $n/b$ partial Grams. BOFT fixes $\lvert\cos\rvert$ between neurons when every $s_i\ne0$ (the cosines themselves when all $s_i$ share a sign), and at butterfly factor $1$ sends each block Gram to its $\mathrm{diag}(s)$-rescaling. HF's default Cayley–Neumann OFT is only approximately orthogonal. The base point is regular.
